c = 0, t = 0.
    Fix η > 0. The total contribution of t > 1 − η, after summing over I, has normalized log at
most 12 log η + B0 sup w + o(1), since ball volumes and density prefactors contribute o(N ). The
lower bound P (u) ≥ −CβB0 follows from Jensen’s inequality, and the desired upper-support
value is at least P (u) − α|d|∥ψ∥∞ . Hence η can be chosen, at the fixed temperature and test
perturbation under consideration, so that this part lies strictly below that value. Dependence
of η on β will have no bearing on ε0 .

changes the row arguments in Euclidean norm by at most Cη K N ∥h∥. Cauchy–Schwarz

    For each center, the restricted log integral is Cw N -Lipschitz in the matrix. Gaussian

put δN = N −(1−2a)/4 . The probability that any centered log integral exceeds N δN in absolute

Conditional on the localized columns, replace the remaining entries by Gaussians using (5.2);
their coefficients are bounded by Cη N −a , so the error is o(N ) uniformly over the centers.
Average this conditional inequality over the localized columns before maximizing over centers.
The maximum is therefore over deterministic, fully averaged expected log partitions; no
maximum of conditional expectations is taken. Then enlarge the remaining spin domain to
the full sphere. Consequently it suffices to bound, for every deterministic sequence of centers,
the unrestricted hybrid expected log partition plus N2 log(1 − t). There is no compactness
assertion uniform in an unspecified growing collection here: if the required bound failed for
the maximum, one could choose a maximizing center for each N and apply the following
subsequence argument to that deterministic sequence. The passage from probability to
expected pressure is legitimate because N −1 log Z is bounded above for these potentials, and
its negative part has uniformly bounded moments by Jensen and (5.1).
    For each localized row, condition on independent fair signs si in the variance-mixture
representation. Its bias is Gaussian of variance t + εD, where
                                           X

These marks and biases are independent from row to row and from the Gaussian remaining
columns. Along a subsequence, t converges and the laws of D converge weakly to a probability
measure supported on [−t, t]. We use t, D for these limits. The next calculation is valid for
any such law; in particular it does not need a lower bound on the number of large coordinates.

comonotone-profile conclusions of the Gaussian cavity argument now apply. Finite-grid limits
are taken before profile approximation, so no concentration bound uniform in cascade depth

5.4   A variance estimate uniform in temperature
The entropy estimate (5.7) gives a loss of at least t/(4χq (0)) when ε ≤ 1/2. We now bound
the possible row gain by C(B0 )εt/χq (0) with no temperature dependence in C(B0 ). The
needed residual estimate is a property of the single-row diffusion.

Lemma 5.2 (Residual moments under a decrease of variance). Let q be a nonnegative
nondecreasing profile with diagonal 1, and let u(z) = −β(−1 − z)2+ /2, β > 0. Put a0 = fz (0, 0)
for its row solution before the root Gaussian variance. Write Eu,v for expectation under the

Proof. For the first inequality put the variance scale into the terminal, so the Gaussian
variance profile is q on the fixed time interval [0, 1]. Let Av and A1 be the derivative solutions
                      √

                                                             √         √

The zeroth-order coefficient lies in [−β, 0], and the drift is Lipschitz with at most linear
growth. The maximum principle gives H ≤ 0. To handle the hinge, convolve the two terminal
derivative functions with the same nonnegative mollifier, preserving their order, and pass
to the limit. When q(0) > 0, mq = 0 before that root variance, so the same comparison
includes the initial heat interval. The derivative martingale at (0, 0) identifies Av (0, 0) with
the expectation on the left of (5.8).
    For the second inequality use variance time, writing the tilted diffusion as

                    dZs = dBs + m(s)a(s, Zs ) ds,               Z0 = 0,        0 ≤ s ≤ v.

The base derivative a = fz is nonnegative, nonincreasing, and convex in position. Nonnegativ-

by differentiating once more: c = fzzz has nonnegative terminal value (a positive measure for
the hinge) and solves the linear equation (∂s + 12 ∂zz + ma∂z )c = −3mfzz c. Smooth concave
approximations with convex first derivative and no far cap justify the claim; their diffusion
limits give the hinge. Thus the drift is nonnegative, nonincreasing, and convex.
    Condition on the Brownian bridge ωs = Bs − (s/v)Bv and write b = Bv ∼ N (0, v),

The lower slope bound is used only for the crossing and approximation; its dependence on β

common Lipschitz bound β. Approximate a general monotone m by steps in L1 ; the linear
PDE difference equation and uniform diffusion moments give local convergence of solutions,
and the flow maps converge on compact endpoint sets. Finally (−1 − Zv )+ ≤ (−1 − Bv )+
supplies uniform integrability for all residual moments. These observations justify the hinge
and coefficient limits in the bridge argument.

Bounded terminal tests. A bounded terminal perturbation changes a tilted endpoint
expectation of a nonnegative observable by at most the factor e2|d|∥ψ∥∞ . On the same realized
cascade and Gaussian field, the normalized Gibbs measure with terminal u + dψ has density
                                 edψ(g)
                                          ∈ [e−2|d|∥ψ∥∞ , e2|d|∥ψ∥∞ ]
                               ⟨edψ(g) ⟩u
relative to the measure with terminal u. Average this pointwise bound over the cascade
and field. The endpoint expectation here is precisely the PDE tilted expectation: for a
bounded continuous terminal direction h, differentiating Φu+λh at zero gives E⟨h(g)⟩u directly,
while differentiating the recursion gives the following linear equation, now denoting the fixed
diagonal by Q:
                          Jt + 12 Jzz + mfz Jz = 0,    J(Q, z) = h(z).
Its diffusion representation identifies the same expectation with the tilted endpoint law. The
initial m = 0 heat interval includes common root variance. For residual diagonal variance,
augment the base measure at each leaf by the residual Gaussian variable and integrate it
inside that leaf weight. Each replica draws a fresh residual variable, even when leaf labels
coincide, so this convention adds no off-diagonal variance. The terminal m = 1 interval is
exactly this convolution. Profile approximation preserves the equality for bounded tests and
the likelihood-ratio bound; monotone truncation extends the latter to nonnegative observables.
In particular there is no factor depending on cascade depth.

The stability radius is uniform in the trial profile, the diagonal in [1/2, 2], and the cap and

mixture law therefore remains valid throughout the later ordered limits. Equations (5.6)–(5.8)
prove the upper support in Theorem 5.1. Combined with (5.4) at d = 0, they prove its
pressure assertion.

5.5   The inner thermodynamic observables
Let µβ,α be the Gaussian tilted endpoint law associated with a minimizer q. The following
argument proves, in particular, that this law does not depend on the choice of minimizer.
Define the random empirical projection measure

                                              1 M
                                                X
                                     LN =          δA y .
                                              M µ=1 µ

Convergence in probability below refers to the joint law of disorder and one Gibbs sample.
Proposition 5.3 (Empirical row law and force normalization). For either the Gaussian law
or any mixture in Theorem 5.1, at fixed β > 0 and 0 < α ≤ B0 , LN converges weakly in
probability to µβ,α . This measure is atomless, assigns positive mass to (−∞, −1), and has
finite moments of its negative part. The gap and normalized-force observables in the problem
have inner N → ∞ limits at every fixed argument, and those limits agree for all these entry
laws.
Proof. First, the normalized random log partition concentrates
                                                     √         for the uncapped potentials
u + dψ. On the convex operator-norm ball ∥A∥op ≤ K N , differentiation gives
                                         √                √          √
                 ∥∇A log Z∥F ≤ β(∥A∥op + M ) + |d|∥ψ ′ ∥∞ M = O( N ).

Extend its restriction from this ball with the same Lipschitz constant and use Gaussian
concentration, through T for mixture entries. The complement has probability exponentially
small in N for a large fixed K, by (5.1). Jensen’s lower bound log Z ≥ −Cβ (M + ∥A∥2op ) −
M |d|∥ψ∥∞ and the corresponding upper bound show that the extension changes its expectation
by o(N ). Hence N −1 log Z − PN,ε (u + dψ) converges to zero in probability.

    Here are direct bounds ensuring that threshold tests and normalization are permitted.

6     Ground energy and the sharp satisfiability threshold
This section separates an energetic issue from a geometric one. The pressure formula
determines when the minimum energy is subextensive. A uniform repair lemma then turns
subextensive energy into exact satisfiability after deleting a fixed fraction of the constraints.
There are two geometric obstacles. Solving for the violated rows can create violations in
other rows, so the repair enlarges the set of rows whose projections it controls. The repaired
vector may then have norm less than one, so a second displacement restores its norm without
spoiling those controlled projections. The negative margin permits radial contraction when
the repaired vector instead has norm at least one.                                     √
    Write A ∈ RM ×N for the pattern matrix, and use unit-spin coordinates y = x/ N . Set

a limit at every fixed (α, β); for every finite B0 , the same limit holds for 0 ≤ ε < ε0 (B0 ) and
0 < α ≤ B0 .

approximate minimizer of the old matrix and then let its approximation error tend to zero.
For every fixed unit y, a fresh projection g = Aµ y has mean zero and variance one, and

                                       EV (g) ≤ 12 E(1 + g)2 = 1.

Thus the expected minimum energy increases by at most one per appended row. After

Approximate minimizers can be selected measurably by taking the first adequate point in a
fixed countable dense subset of the sphere, since HA is continuous. If M < N , the row kernel
contains a unit vector, at which every gap is one. Hence e(α) = 0 for α < 1; continuity gives
e(1) = 0.

6.2   Uniform geometry of the rows
The repair needs a uniformly bounded right inverse for every set of slightly fewer than N
rows. It also needs a bound on how many rows can be close to contact at an arbitrary spin.
The next lemma supplies both facts before the spin is selected from the random matrix. This
uniformity is what permits us to apply it at a ground minimizer.

Lemma 6.3 (Uniform row estimates). Fix B0 < ∞ and 0 < ρ < 1/8. There are constants
K < ∞, c > 0, u0 > 0, a > 0, and C < ∞, depending only on (B0 , ρ), such that, for all
sufficiently large N , all M ≤ B0 N , and every 0 ≤ ε ≤ 1/2, the following hold simultaneously
with probability at least 1 − Ce−aN :

    The following elementary fact supplies a direction with uniformly small row projections.
It does not require any randomness.

Lemma 6.4 (A direction inside finitely many slabs). Let W be a D-dimensional Euclidean
space, D ≥ 1, and L : W → Rm a linear map. There exists a unit v ∈ W such that
                                                ∥L∥
                                        ∥Lv∥∞ ≤ √ .                                         (6.6)
                                                 D
Proof. If L has a nonzero kernel, take a unit kernel vector. Otherwise the set Q = {w ∈ W :
∥Lw∥∞ ≤ 1} is compact and has nonempty interior. Let w∗ maximize ∥w∥ over Q. The active
linear functionals, those satisfying |(Lw∗ )j | = 1, span W ∗ . Indeed, if they had a common
nonzero annihilator h, both w∗ + th and w∗ − th would remain in Q for sufficiently small
t > 0: active inequalities would be unchanged and the finitely many inactive inequalities have
positive slack. But at least one of these two points has squared norm at least ∥w∗ ∥2 + t2 ∥h∥2 ,
a contradiction. There are consequently at least D distinct active rows, and

                                  D ≤ ∥Lw∗ ∥2 ≤ ∥L∥2 ∥w∗ ∥2 .

The vector v = w∗ /∥w∗ ∥ satisfies the required bound.

Proposition 6.6 (Subextensive parent energy implies satisfiability). Let 0 < α < α′ ≤ B0 ,
and let the entries have any one of the laws νε , 0 ≤ ε ≤ 1/2. If
                                   1
                                     E min H⌊α′ N ⌋×N −→ 0,
                                   N
then a matrix with ⌊αN ⌋ rows is satisfiable with probability tending to one.

                                                55
Proof. Deleting rows serves one precise purpose: it reduces the number of small or negative
gaps from at most slightly more than N to strictly fewer than N . The remaining rows can
then be repaired using the surplus dimension in Lemma 6.5.
    The constants are chosen in the following order. First fix η ∈ (0, 1 − α/α′ ), so that for all
sufficiently large N the retained row count m = ⌊αN ⌋ is at most (1 − η)M , where M = ⌊α′ N ⌋.
Next choose 0 < ρ < min(1/8, η/8) and obtain K, c, u0 from Lemma 6.3 for the parent matrix.
Finally fix d > 0 satisfying (6.7) and d2 /u20 ≤ ρ/2.
    Choose a measurable approximate minimizer y of the parent matrix, with energy√ at
most min HA + N −1 . Markov’s inequality and the hypothesis show that ∥rA (y)∥ ≤ d N
with probability tending to one. On the uniform geometric event, the set of problematic
parent rows Spar = {µ : 1 + Aµ y ≤ u0 } has size at most (1 + ρ)N . Indeed, the rows with
|1 + Aµ y| ≤ u0 number at most (1 + ρ/2)N , while those with 1 + Aµ y < −u0 number at most
∥rA (y)∥2 /u20 ≤ ρN/2.
    Independently of the parent matrix, retain a uniformly chosen set I of exactly m rows.
Conditional on the parent and y, the random variable X = |I ∩ Spar | is hypergeometric, with

                         E[X | A, y] ≤ (1 − η)(1 + ρ)N ≤ (1 − 7ρ)N.

Writing X as a sum of inclusion indicators gives, with k = |Spar | and r = m/M ,
                                                 M −k  k  (1 + ρ)N
                     Var(X | A, y) = kr(1 − r)        ≤ ≤          .
                                                 M −1  4      4
Here the covariance of two distinct inclusion indicators is −r(1 − r)/(M − 1); the formula is
also immediate when k = 0. Chebyshev’s inequality therefore gives
                                                            1+ρ

                                                           100ρ2 N
Thus X ≤ (1 − 2ρ)N with probability tending to one, uniformly over the parent on the stated
event.
    The retained matrix inherits the parent’s operator-norm and surjection bounds, and its
residual at y is no larger than the parent residual. Lemma 6.5 makes it exactly feasible on
the unit sphere. Finally, an independently chosen subset of rows of an iid matrix, listed in
increasing order, has precisely the law of an iid m × N matrix. This proves the assertion for
the desired row count.

6.4   The common threshold and subsequent ordered limits

the choice of B0 above, for every fixed 0 ≤ ε < ε0 (B0 ), the number αJ in (6.3) satisfies

Proof. If α < αJ , choose a fixed α′ ∈ (α, αJ ). The expected parent ground energy is o(N )
for both laws by Lemma 6.1 and pressure universality. Proposition 6.6 proves satisfiability
with probability tending to one.
    For αJ < α ≤ B0 , the same ground-energy limit is strictly positive. The map A 7→ R(A)

                       ∥rA (y)∥ − ∥rA′ (y)∥ ≤ ∥(A − A′ )y∥ ≤ ∥A − A′ ∥F ,

Corollary 6.8 (Transfer of the remaining ordered limits). Fix 0 ≤ ε < ε0 (B0 ). On a full
interval above the common threshold, the thermodynamic gap and normalized-force observables
at every fixed positive temperature and fixed argument agree with their Gaussian counterparts.
Consequently each further ordered limit in the problem exists for the mixture law if and only
if it exists for the Gaussian law, and their values agree whenever it exists.

Proof. The fixed-temperature row-marginal result in Section 5 gives equality of the inner
N → ∞ limits for every fixed β, argument, and α ∈ (αJ , B0 ]. Limits of these identical
functions, taken successively in the prescribed order, have identical existence and values.
This assertion makes no exchange of limits; existence of the Gaussian zero-temperature and
jamming limits is established in the following sections.


7      Zero temperature and mechanical bounds
The pressure formula supplies variational minimizers at each positive temperature. We now
extract their zero-temperature limits and obtain the mechanical estimates that will control
the subsequent approach to the threshold. There are two endpoints to distinguish: the
diffusion immediately before the concentrated terminal layer, and the physical row gap after
that layer. We first derive their relation. A supporting functional then identifies the negative
part of the physical law with ground-minimizer residuals. Finally the contact count, spectral
estimate, and small-gap bounds give control uniform as the density approaches the threshold.
Section 8 will use that control to preserve mass and moments in the critical limit.
    Throughout this section the rows are Gaussian and αJ < α ≤ B0 is fixed. Write
e = e(α) > 0. Constants with a subscript e may depend on this fixed density; constants
called uniform are uniform on a fixed bounded interval of UNSAT densities adjacent to αJ .
All assertions about a random matrix are in probability as N → ∞, at fixed values of every
cutoff occurring in the assertion. No uniqueness of the limiting variational parameters is
assumed in this section.

7.1     Compactness of the variational parameters
Let qβ be any minimizer in (4.9). Denote its variance-time row solution by fβ ; thus, in the

      (fβ )t = − 21 (fβ )zz + mqβ (t)(fβ )2z ,

measure therefore retains an endpoint mass, denoted by ∆ below; mass from just below qβ,∗
can also contribute to it.

terminal convergence with a common quadratic growth bound. Applying this at T < 1, using

compact time intervals before 1. The same argument constructs, and proves uniqueness of,
the stated solution for an integrable γ.

7.2   Endpoint laws and the negative marginal
Proposition 7.3 (Subsequential endpoint law). Let Z0 = 0 and dZt = γ(t)gz (t, Zt ) dt + dBt ,
and put H = 1 + Z1 . Along the subsequence defining (γ, ∆), the tilted row gap converges in

assign positive probability to every nonempty open interval. This statement is subsequential;
uniqueness is addressed separately below and in Section 8.
Proof. On each constant-CDF interval the row tilt is the Gaussian Doob transform. Com-
posing these transforms, then approximating a general CDF, identifies its physical gap with
1 + Z1β , where
                                              Z t

Couple Z β and Z with the same Brownian motion. Before a fixed time T < 1, the coefficient
and gradient convergence above and Gronwall’s inequality imply convergence in expected
supremum √ norm. On [T, 1], replace the gradient by RUβ,z . The error has expected size at
most Ce 1 − T , by (7.4) and the uniform bound on γβ ; the Brownian increment has the
same bound. The resulting deterministic flow fixes positive gaps and multiplies a negative
gap between times T and t by (1 + χ̄β (t))/(1 + χ̄β (T )). First send β → ∞, and then T ↑ 1.
The limiting integrable drift on [T, 1] has vanishing total mass, whereas the total mass
concentrating near time 1 in γβ (t) dt is ∆. Since χ̄β (1) = 0 and χ̄β (T ) → χ̄(T ), the displayed
flow factor tends to (1 + ∆)−1 as β → ∞ and then T ↑ 1. This proves (7.5). Since the drift
is nonnegative, the negative part of the gap is bounded by (1 + B1 )− ; this gives uniform
integrability of every moment of the negative part.
    For atomlessness, condition on the Brownian bridge RB       et = Bt − tB1 . The map from
b = B1 to Z1 is increasing and has slope between exp(− γ) and 1. Indeed its variation
satisfies J ′ (t) = 1 + γ(t)gzz (t, Zt )J(t), J(0) = 0. Approximation proves the same bounds
when derivatives exist only almost everywhere. The conditional endpoint law is thus the
pushforward of a Gaussian under a globally bi-Lipschitz map. That map is onto R, so the
law has a bounded density and assigns positive probability to every nonempty open interval.
The piecewise linear map in (7.5) preserves these properties.

    The variable H is the pre-layer gap; h∗ is the gap sampled by the microscopic observable.

                                                      63
7.4   Mechanical contacts and uniform cutoff estimates
We now relate these variational quantities to finite-dimensional minimizers. Three separate
facts are needed. A positive-energy minimizer has at least N strictly violated rows and
almost surely no row at exactly zero gap. A uniform small-residual bound then prevents an
extensive number of those violated rows from disappearing at zero in the large-N limit. A
different bound, proved on near-ground configurations later in the section, controls small
positive gaps in the prescribed thermal order. Write Πy = I − yy T , ri (y) = (−1 − Ai y)+ , and
∇S H(y) = Πy ∇H(y) on the unit sphere.

Lemma 7.6 (Mechanical contact count). Almost surely, every positive-energy Gaussian
ground minimizer has no zero gap outside its active set C = {i : ri > 0}, and k = |C| ≥ N .
At such a minimizer,
                                X
 ATC (AC y + 1) = λy,      λ=         ri (1 + ri ) > 0,   v T (ATC AC − λI)v ≥ 0   (v ⊥ y). (7.11)
                                i∈C

Proof. For a fixed C, Gaussian genericity gives simple nonzero singular values of AC and a
nonzero component of ATC 1 in each corresponding right singular direction. These properties
hold almost surely, since their exceptional sets are proper algebraic sets. Stationarity implies
the first identity in (7.11); its scalar product with y gives λ > 0. The genericity just noted
precludes λ from being an eigenvalue of ATC AC . Thus the stationary vector is obtained by
inversion. Its unit-norm equation is rational in λ, is not identically satisfied (it tends to
zero at infinity), and has finitely many roots. Conditional on AC , any row outside C almost
surely avoids gap zero at all these finitely many vectors. A union over the finitely many sets
C proves the first assertion, even if such a row was initially allowed to have zero gap. The
energy is now smooth near the minimizer, so its constrained Hessian is nonnegative on the
tangent space. Finally stationarity with λ > 0 puts y in the row span of AC . A nontrivial
kernel of AC would be tangent and would have Hessian value −λ, a contradiction. Hence
rank AC = N , and k ≥ N .
                                                                                           √
    We record the common counting estimate behind the cutoff bounds. Work on ∥A∥ ≤ K N ,
whose probability tends to one for fixed sufficiently large K = K(B0 ). Both the residual map
and the tangent gradient obey
                           √
      ∥r(y) − r(y ′ )∥2 ≤ K N ∥y − y ′ ∥,     ∥∇S H(y) − ∇S H(y ′ )∥ ≤ CN ∥y − y ′ ∥.     (7.12)

At a deterministic unit vector y0 , condition on all scalar projections Ai y0 . The tangent row
components remain independent standard Gaussians, and therefore

                          ∇S H(y0 ) | (Ai y0 )i ∼ N 0, ∥r(y0 )∥22 IN −1 .

The upper bound requires no identification of positive gap tests at exact ground minimizers.
Proof. Remove a tested row. Its insertion denominator is at most one, whereas its penalty
vanishes on the tested positive interval. Conditional on the cavity spin, the removed projection
is standard Gaussian. Its density is bounded below on [−1, 0], which proves the lower bound.
The same proof works for the small mixtures, conditional on their variances, with a uniform
lower density bound.
    For the upper bound, exact ground minimizers alone are insufficient: the observable first
takes N → ∞ at positive temperature. We therefore prove the counting bound throughout
a thin energy sublevel and only then concentrate the Gibbs measure on it. The needed
replacement for the exact contact count k ≥ N is a lower bound k ≥ (1 − 2η ′ )N on that
whole sublevel. We derive it before counting the positive gaps.

counting event included all positive gaps in (0, u]. At fixed density, the finite-temperature
row marginal and Proposition 7.3 converge along the chosen subsequence at both endpoints
0 and u, which are atomless. Thus the bound passes directly to every subsequential law of
h∗ , without removing a positive lower cutoff or identifying positive tests at exact ground
minimizers.

                                                     68
Proof. The denominator is positive.    The diffusion representation writes g(0, 0) as a negative
terminal expectation minus 12 E γgz2 ; atomlessness with a positive density on negative intervals
                                R

makes the terminal expectation strictly negative. Recovery trials in Proposition 7.2 remain

    To verify the normalization assertion, scale a fixed pair by c > 0. If gc is the solution for
(cγ, c∆), then cgc solves the equation with coefficient γ and terminal value −V /(∆ + 1/c).
The ratio for the scaled pair is therefore the ratio for this rescaled equation. It decreases as
c ↑ ∞ and converges to the ratio with terminal −V /∆, by the PDE comparison and stability
already proved. Every shape can be normalized to χ̄(0) = 1, and every normalized limiting
ratio is approached by its finite scalings. The two infima are equal.
    The next section uses the susceptibility bounds of Lemma 7.8 and the two direct cutoff
estimates to construct the normalized critical law. Joint convexity will first supply the
fixed-density uniqueness still missing for positive gaps.


8     A convex critical problem and the limiting microscopic laws
At a fixed UNSAT density, Section 7 describes subsequential zero-temperature laws by a
susceptibility and its PDE. We first prove that this description is unique. We then approach
the threshold, retaining the notation αc = αJ , and construct a unique normalized pair
consisting of a susceptibility χ and a nonnegative terminal force variable R. The limiting
state H1 will be the gap variable: H1 ≥ 0, and RH1 = 0 separates positive gaps from contacts.
     There are two different convergence questions. Convexity on one Wiener space iden-
tifies every weak subsequential limit, but does not preserve contact probabilities or force
normalization. To obtain those facts we prove that the critical susceptibility vanishes at the
endpoint, upgrade the terminal convergence to L2 , and apply the uniform small-gap and
small-force estimates from the preceding section. The resulting laws, with the limits taken in
the prescribed order, are stated in Theorem 8.12.
     The stochastic variational viewpoint is related to Boué–Dupuis [4] and Auffinger–Chen [2].
More specifically, the use of terminal duality to obtain a variational formula over Brownian
martingales, with the optimizer identified by the derivative of the Parisi PDE along its
diffusion, has a close analogue in Mourrat [17, Lemma 2.2 and Theorem 2 in the preprint];
see also Chen, Issa and Mourrat [7, Proposition 8.1 and Theorem 1.5]. Proposition 8.1
establishes the representation needed here for a concave quadratic terminal function and a
nonnegative terminal variable in L2 . Rewriting its quadratic terms using s = 1/χ gives the
joint convexity in Lemma 8.2. The possibility that χ vanishes at the endpoint requires the
additional compactness and equality arguments below; the critical uniqueness statement is
not a direct consequence of the cited results.

8.1    The probability space and the admissible class
Fix canonical Wiener space, with its completed natural filtration (Ft )0≤t≤1 and coordinate
Brownian motion B. Realize each fixed-density zero-temperature optimizer’s forward diffusion
as the strong solution driven by this same B. Thus the terminal variables for different
densities will belong to one Hilbert space, rather than merely having laws that can be
compared. Expectations in this section are on that space. For every R ∈ L2 (F1 ), martingale
representation gives a unique predictable b ∈ L2 (Ω × [0, 1]) such that

8.2    Duality and positive-energy uniqueness
For fixed χ, quadratic duality replaces the PDE value by a minimum over terminal force
variables. Passing from χ to its reciprocal s then makes the susceptibility and terminal
variable jointly convex. This change of variables is what allows two physical optimizers to be
compared on the Wiener space just fixed.

Lemma 8.2 (Convexity and its equality cases). The set of admissible s is convex, as is its
normalized part s(0) = 1. The functional Jα,λ is jointly convex in (R, s). If two pairs attain
equality in this convexity inequality at a nontrivial convex combination, then
               c1       c2          b1,t     b2,t
                     =        ,           =           for dt ⊗ P-almost every (t, ω).       (8.4)
              s1 (0)   s2 (0)      s1 (t)   s2 (t)

If λ > 0, equality also forces R1 = R2 in L2 .
Proof. For 0 < q < 1, the reciprocal of qs1 + (1 − q)s2 is the weighted harmonic mean
H(x, y) = (q/x + (1 − q)/y)−1 evaluated at (χ1 , χ2 ). This function is increasing in each
coordinate and concave on the positive quadrant: differentiation gives a negative semidefinite
Hessian. Hence H(χ1 (t), χ2 (t)) is concave and nonincreasing in t. It extends continuously
to the endpoint, and its negative derivative is nonnegative, nondecreasing, and integrable.
Integrability of the new s is immediate from its definition as a convex combination. The
same argument preserves L2 integrability and the normalization.
    For a real Hilbert space H, the perspective (x, u) 7→ ∥x∥2H /u, u > 0, is convex. Its
convexity defect vanishes exactly when x1 /u1 = x2 /u2 . Apply this once with H = R to c,
and for almost every t with H = L2 (Ω) to bt . Integration preserves nonnegative defects, so
equality in the sum implies the asserted almost-everywhere equalities. The remaining terms
are linear, except for αλER2 /2, whose strict convexity gives the last assertion.

Corollary 8.3 (Uniqueness in the UNSAT phase). At each fixed α > αc the minimizing
physical susceptibility in (7.3) is unique. Thus the entire zero-temperature endpoint law in
(7.5), including its restriction to positive gaps, is unique.
Proof. Two minimizing pairs have the same R and therefore the same c and b. For a PDE
optimizer, bt = fhh (t, Ht ) < 0 almost surely for t < 1. To see the strict inequality, the
curvature is nonpositive, and its differentiated diffusion representation propagates the strictly
negative terminal curvature on (−∞, 0). On every interval ending before time 1 the drift is
Lipschitz, its flow derivative is positive, and the diffusion has positive probability of reaching
that interval. Equivalently the strong maximum principle applied after smoothing gives
strict negativity; the representation retains strictness on removing the smoothing. Equality
in (8.4) therefore gives s1 = s2 almost everywhere, hence everywhere before the endpoint
by continuity. Their continuous susceptibilities have the same endpoint value as well. The
PDE and its forward diffusion are then identical. The subsequential description in (7.5)
consequently has only one possible limit.

Proposition 8.4 (Existence of a normalized critical pair). The functional Jαc ,0 is nonneg-
ative on the whole admissible class, without a normalization constraint. Every sequence of
normalized physical pairs with αn ↓ αc has a subsequence such that

              χn → χ uniformly on every [0, T ], T < 1,            Rn ⇀ R in L2 (F1 ).

Its limit is normalized, satisfies s ∈ L2 (0, 1) and c ≥ c0 , and attains Jαc ,0 (R, s) = 0.
   We will prove uniqueness for the limits supplied by this proposition. That conclusion
concerns physical limits; it will not require a classification of all admissible zero-value pairs.

Proof. Nonnegativity of the limiting functional. First suppose ∆ > 0. For A > 0, direct
substitution gives the exact homogeneity identity
                                                                   α
                            AJα,1 (R/A, s/A) = Jα,0 (R, s) +         ER2 .                     (8.7)
                                                                  2A
The reciprocal of s/A is Aχ, so this is an admissible zero-temperature trial. At αc , zero
ground energy and Proposition 8.1 make its value nonnegative. Letting A → ∞ proves
the assertion. If ∆ = 0, replace χ by χ + η. The quadratic term converges by dominated
convergence, while 1/(χ + η) ↑ 1/χ; let η ↓ 0.
    Compactness on the common space. Normalization and concavity give 1 ≥ χn (t) ≥ 1 − t.
On each [0, T ] their slopes are bounded by a constant depending only on T : monotonicity
of γn gives γn (t) ≤ (1 − t)−1 . Arzelà–Ascoli and a diagonal selection give the stated local

                                                      72
uniform convergence. The limit is concave and nonincreasing; its continuous endpoint value
is ∆ = limt↑1 χ(t), which is not initially identified with limn χn (1). Its derivative gives an
admissible integrable γ = −χ′ . At differentiability points of χ, the concavity inequalities
imply γn → γ; the same local bound then gives L1 (0, T ) convergence for every T < 1. Fatou’s
lemma gives s ∈ L2 .
    The bound on Rn gives a weakly convergent subsequence on the fixed Wiener space.
The cone of nonnegative L2 variables is weakly closed. Martingale representation, being a
bounded linear isometry after the constant component is included, gives weak convergence of
bn and convergence of cn . In particular c ≥ c0 .
    Passage to the minimum. For each T < 1, uniform convergence of the positive weights
χn on [0, T ] and weak convergence of bn imply
                             Z T                           Z T
                                   χ(t)Eb2t dt ≤ lim inf         χn (t)Eb2n,t dt.
                              0                     n       0

8.4   Interior PDE representation
A weak terminal limit need not by itself retain the PDE representation. We now recover
that representation before comparing two critical limits. The strict curvature and the time
regularity proved here will make the common-noise uniqueness argument possible.
    For the normalized physical pairs use gap coordinates and denote the scaled PDE by fn .
Its equation and terminal data are

χ(1) = 0:

Lemma 8.5 (Interior limits and strict curvature). For each subsequence in Proposition 8.4,
fn and an converge locally uniformly on [0, 1) × R to functions f and a = fh determined by
χ. They satisfy (8.8) with λn + χn replaced by χ. They are smooth in the spatial variable
in the interior and locally Lipschitz in time there, together with each spatial derivative used
below. For t < 1,

                    fhh (t, h) < 0,        fhhh (t, h) > 0,             lim a(t, h) = 0.
                                                                    h→+∞


                                                      73
On the common Wiener space the limiting martingale and diffusion obey
                      Z t
     Ht = 1 + B t +         γ(v)rv dv,   rt = a(t, Ht ),    bt = fhh (t, Ht ),     t < 1.     (8.9)
                       0

Both Ht and rt extend continuously and in L2 to t = 1, with r1 = R.

Proof. Comparison estimates. The curvature bounds are parabolic comparison bounds.
Specifically, if b = (fn )hh , then bt + 12 bhh + γn an bh + γn b2 = 0. The spatially constant
function −(λn + χn (t))−1 solves this equation, and zero is the upper comparison solution.
Differentiating once more gives the linear equation dt + 12 dhh + γn an dh + 3γn bd = 0 for
d = (fn )hhh , which preserves nonnegativity backwards from the convex terminal derivative.
These comparisons follow first for smooth terminal data and smooth coefficients; the bounds
survive approximation. The first-order solution is qn (t, h) = −h2− /(2(λn + χn (t))). Its missing
diffusion term is bounded in absolute value by sn (t)/2. Comparison with qn ± 12 t1 sn proves
                                                                                     R
                                                                                   q R
the value bound. The derivative bound follows by taking secants of length ( t1 sn )/sn (t)
and using the curvature bound; the zero-length limiting case is immediate.
    Convergence with a terminal cutoff. At a fixed cutoff T < 1 the value estimates compare
fn (T, ·) and fm (T, ·) with quadratic functions whose coefficients converge.
                                                                         √       The error left
after this quadratic comparison is bounded uniformly in space by C 1 − T , uniformly in
n, m. On [0, T ], the difference w = fn − fm solves a linear equation with drift γn (an + am )/2
and source (γn − γm )a2m /2. The drift has uniformly bounded Lipschitz and linear-growth
constants on that interval, and its diffusion has uniformly bounded second moments on
bounded starting sets. Its Feynman–Kac representation propagates a constant terminal error
without amplification. The quadratic terminal error tends to zero at fixed T , as does the
source error because γn → γ in L1 (0, T ). For each fixed T , first let n, m →√∞: only the
constant terminal error remains, bounded on every earlier compact set by C 1 − T . Then
let T ↑ 1. This proves the Cauchy property and shows that the limit depends only on χ.
Curvature bounds and secants give convergence of the first derivatives.
    Regularity for a measurable time coefficient. On a compact time interval ending strictly
before 1, γ is bounded. We need spatial smoothness and local time-Lipschitz bounds, including
at jumps of γ, for the inverse-curvature map used below. We give the regularity argument in
a form that does not require continuity of this coefficient in time. Reverse time and multiply
a by smooth space and time cutoffs supported in a larger interior rectangle, with the time
cutoff zero at its initial edge. The resulting inhomogeneous heat equation has bounded source:
the nonlinear term is γaah , and the cutoff terms involve only a and ah , already bounded on
that rectangle. For the heat semigroup Pu , differentiation of the Gaussian kernel gives

       ∥∂h Pu F ∥∞ ≤ Cu−1/2 ∥F ∥∞ ,       ∥∂hh Pu F ∥∞ ≤ Cη u−1+η/2 [F ]C η ,    0 < η < 1.
                                                                            h


The second estimate uses the zero integral of the differentiated kernel to subtract F (h). The
first estimate and its spatial increment version in Duhamel’s formula give a uniform spatial
C 1,η bound for every η < 1: splitting the time integral at the square of the spatial increment
bounds the gradient increment by a constant times |z|(1 + | log |z||). The source is therefore
                                                                                          ′
spatially C η . The second displayed estimate is integrable at u = 0 and yields C 2,η on a
                                                                                    m,η
smaller rectangle for 0 < η ′ < η. More generally, if a is already bounded in Ch , m ≥ 1,
then the localized source is bounded in Chm−1,η : its derivatives of order m − 1 use derivatives
                                                                                                ′
of a of order at most m. Apply the second heat-kernel estimate to ∂hm−1 F to obtain Chm+1,η
on a smaller rectangle. This induction uses no derivative of a that it is trying to establish. It
gives every spatial derivative on nested interior rectangles. Multiplication by the bounded
function γ(t) preserves all spatial Hölder bounds, so this bootstrap is valid for measurable

                                                74
time coefficients. Smooth coefficient approximation justifies the intermediate differentiations
with these same bounds. Finally the PDE bounds the almost-everywhere time derivative of
every spatial derivative on smaller rectangles. This gives the claimed local time-Lipschitz
property, including across jumps of γ. The uniform spatial Hölder and time-Lipschitz bounds
make these derivatives jointly continuous. At the start of this argument the source uses
the bounded weak derivative ah supplied by the curvature estimate; the distributional PDE
passes from an by local uniform convergence and L1 convergence of γn . Thus the initial
Duhamel representation does not presume smoothness.
    Strict curvature and identification of the martingale. The value bound on h > 0 and a ≥ 0
imply a(t, h) → 0 as h → +∞. On the negative half-line, (8.8) makes a unbounded. Thus
a is not affine, its nonnegative second derivative is nontrivial at every later interior time,
and its nonpositive first derivative is nontrivial there. The strong maximum principle, on an
interval between two strictly interior times, gives fhhh > 0 and fhh < 0 at the earlier time.
    The coupled forward diffusions converge in L2 uniformly on each compact time interval, by
the local gradient convergence, L1 convergence of γn , and the common linear-growth bounds.
For precision, those deterministic growth bounds and Gronwall’s inequality give uniformly
bounded moments of every fixed finite order for supt≤T |Hn,t | and supt≤T |an (t, Hn,t )|, for
each T < 1. The fourth-moment bound permits spatial localization to be removed in L2
after the coupled convergence is proved on bounded sets. Consequently an (t, Hn,t ) → a(t, Ht )
strongly in L2 for every t < 1. But an (t, Hn,t ) = E[Rn | Ft ] converges weakly to E[R | Ft ].
This identifies rt ; Itô’s formula identifies bt = fhh (t, Ht ) and proves (8.9). Finally Doob’s
inequality gives E supt≤1 |rt |2 ≤ 4ER2 . Since γ ∈ L1 , the drift integral in (8.9) converges
both almost surely and in L2 at 1. The martingale itself has its continuous L2 extension with
terminal value R.

8.5   Uniqueness at zero energy
Proposition 8.6 (Uniqueness of the normalized critical limit). Any two subsequential limits
supplied by Proposition 8.4 coincide on the common Wiener space. In particular the normalized
susceptibility and the law of (H1 , R) are independent of the density sequence.
Proof. Equality identifies the normalized curvatures along the states. Both pairs minimize the
globally nonnegative critical functional. Their midpoint is admissible and also has value zero.
Lemma 8.2 and si (0) = 1 yield c1 = c2 =: c > 0 and χ1 b1 = χ2 b2 almost everywhere. Put
ki (t, h) = −χi (t)(fi )hh (t, h). The proof uses this matched normalized curvature to express one
state process as a deterministic transform of the other. Their common Brownian noise forces
the transform to have spatial derivative one; the common martingale mean then identifies
the susceptibilities. Interior continuity upgrades the curvature identity to
                            k1 (t, H1,t ) = k2 (t, H2,t ),   0 < t < 1,
on a single event of probability one. Each ki (t, ·) is strictly decreasing with strictly negative
derivative by Lemma 8.5. Each Hi,t has full support in R: on every interior compact time
interval its additive-noise diffusion has locally bounded Lipschitz drift, and localized Girsanov
equivalence, or the Brownian support property followed by continuity of the solution map,
reaches every open interval. Thus the support of ki (t, Hi,t ) is the closure of the range of
ki (t, ·). Equality of their laws identifies the two ranges, which are open intervals. We may
therefore define
                        Ψ(t, h) = k2 (t, ·)−1 (k1 (t, h)), H2,t = Ψ(t, H1,t ).
These inverse functions have uniform local bounds. At a fixed (t0 , h0 ) put y0 = Ψ(t0 , h0 ).
Strict decrease gives
                        k2 (t0 , y0 − 1) > k1 (t0 , h0 ) > k2 (t0 , y0 + 1).

                                                   75
Joint continuity preserves these inequalities on a neighborhood of (t0 , h0 ), trapping Ψ between
y0 − 1 and y0 + 1. Compactness and uniqueness of the inverse give continuity there. The
negative derivative of k2 is bounded away from zero on a slightly smaller compact rectangle.
The implicit derivative Ψh (t, h) = (k1 )h (t, h)/(k2 )h (t, Ψ(t, h)) is therefore jointly continuous,
all needed spatial derivatives are bounded locally, and the mean-value theorem with the
time-Lipschitz bounds on k1 , k2 proves a uniform local time-Lipschitz bound on Ψ. Finite
covers give the same assertions on every compact subset of (0, 1) × R.
    The common noise forces a spatial translation. Taking quadratic covariations with the
driving Brownian motion on an arbitrary interval [u, t] ⊂ (0, 1) gives
                                                            Z t
                        t − u = [H2 , B]t − [H2 , B]u =           Ψh (v, H1,v ) dv.
                                                             u

This chain rule needs no continuous time derivative. After localization, the temporal incre-
ments in a partition contribute at most C ∆t |∆B|, which tends to zero. The spatial Taylor
                                            P

remainders contribute at most C max |∆B| |∆H1 |2 , which tends to zero in probability,
                                                P

since quadratic-variation sums of a continuous semimartingale are tight. The first-order sums
give the displayed integral. Rational interval endpoints and continuity give all these identities
on one event of probability one. It follows that Ψh (t, H1,t ) = 1 for almost every time, almost
surely. Full support gives Ψh (t, h) = 1 for every h at almost every t; continuity extends it to
every interior t. Hence Ψ(t, h) = h + d(t).
    The martingale mean identifies the susceptibility. The equality of k’s now holds for all h.
Integrate the matched curvatures from h to +∞, using gradient decay there, to obtain

                                 χ1 (t)a1 (t, h) = χ2 (t)a2 (t, h + d(t)).

Along the states this gives χ1 r1,t = χ2 r2,t . Taking expectations yields cχ1 = cχ2 , so χ1 = χ2 .
The PDE is determined by χ, and the two forward diffusions have the same initial condition
and Brownian motion. They agree, and their martingales, including their L2 terminal values,
agree as well.

8.6    Variational conditions and the vanishing endpoint mass
From now on, “the critical pair” means the unique limit of the normalized physical optimizers
just constructed. To reach the endpoint we must show that ∆ = χ(1) vanishes. The
convergence so far is local in time: even a vanishing sequence of endpoint values χn (1) would
not exclude mass of γn concentrating near 1. We instead use optimality of the critical pair in
the full admissible class.
    The first-order conditions are expressed by the stationarity defect J(t) and its integral
K(t), defined by

Jα,λ , and P, I, J extend continuously to 1. In the interior, P ′ = αc Eb2t is continuous. Choose
the right-continuous nondecreasing representative of γ. Let ν = dγ denote the nonnegative
Stieltjes measure on [0, 1) with ν([0, t]) = γ(t); this convention includes a possible initial
value of γ as an atom at zero.

positive constant times 1 − t. This contradicts s ∈ L2 . Thus in this case interior support
points approach 1, and continuity of J again gives J(1) = 0.

8.7   Strong convergence through the endpoint
We have now identified the only possible weak limit and proved χ(1) = 0. The remaining
analytic task is to strengthen convergence of the terminal force. A trial obtained by rescaling
the critical minimizer bounds the physical normalization in terms of α − αc ; comparison at
that normalization then yields convergence of the full L2 norm. Positivity probabilities will
be treated separately using the cutoff bounds.




                                                            78
Proposition 8.9 (Strong critical convergence). As αn ↓ αc , the normalized physical variables

             Rn → R in L2 ,       γn → γ in L1 (0, 1),     Hn,1 → H1 in L2 .

8.8   Contacts, atoms, and the prescribed order of limits
Strong L2 convergence preserves the first two moments, but a positive variable may still
converge to zero. To identify contacts we therefore use both uniform cutoff estimates from
Section 7: the force estimate preserves P(R > 0), and the positive-gap estimate identifies
P(H1 = 0). Only after these masses are known can we normalize the limiting force law.
Lemma 8.10 (Contact and noncontact mass). At the critical pair,
                                           1
         P(R > 0) = P(H1 = 0) =               ,        {R > 0} = {H1 = 0}   modulo null sets.
                                           αc

                                                         79
Proof. Let pn = P(Rn > 0). Corollary 7.9 gives αn pn → 1 and the direct cutoff bound

                                         lim sup P(0 < Rn ≤ u) = 0
                                         u↓0   n

after discarding a finite initial part of the density sequence. Apply strong convergence at
positive continuity cutoffs and then send the cutoff to zero. This gives P(R > 0) = limn pn =
1/αc .
    Similarly the direct bound in (7.16), passed to the atomless endpoint law at each fixed
UNSAT density, gives limu↓0 supn P(0 < h∗n ≤ u) = 0. Its constants and its upper cutoff
are uniform in the density. This is a bound on the whole interval (0, u] at fixed density;
no interchange between a lower gap cutoff and the density limit is used. At every positive
continuity point u of the law of H1 , the L2 convergence h∗n → H1 gives convergence of the
probabilities of (−∞, u]. Subtracting the negative mass pn and then sending u ↓ 0 proves
P(H1 = 0) = 1/αc ; fixed-density endpoint laws have no atom at zero, as established in (7.5).
Complementarity gives the inclusion {R > 0} ⊂ {H1 = 0}. Equality of their probabilities
proves the final assertion.

    The contact atom at zero is now identified. To pass the microscopic observables at every
positive cutoff, we still need to exclude atoms away from zero. The two variables require
different estimates: nondegeneracy in deterministic variance time for R, and a bounded state
drift away from zero for H1 .

On |r−x| ≤ x/2 both terms are nonpositive. Off this interval the second is at most −x2 /(32ℓ2 )
and the first at most 1/(32ℓ), so their sum is nonpositive when ℓ ≤ x2 . Choose δ > 0 so that
v0 = T − δ lies in that final region and 2δ ≤ x2 . For η 2 ≤ δ one has ℓ ≤ 2δ. At this fixed η,
Φ and its stochastic derivative are bounded, so Itô’s formula gives

                               EΦ(T, R) ≤ EΦ(v0 , rt(v0 ) ) ≤ δ −ζ/2 ,

uniformly in η. Since Φ(T, R) ≥ e−a0 η −ζ on |R − x| ≤ η,

                                        P(|R − x| ≤ η) ≤ Cx η ζ .

This proves the first assertion.
   The gap variable. We show that the state drift is bounded on spatial intervals away from
the contact point. Positivity of the drift and monotonicity of a imply, for t < t′ < 1,

                            a(t, h) = Ea(t′ , Htt,h        ′
                                                 ′ ) ≤ Ea(t , h + Bt′ −t ).


Fix x > 0, write τ = 1 − t, and set tj = 1 − 2−j τ and xj = x/2 + (x/2)2−j/4 . Split the
last expectation according to whether the Brownian increment is below −(xj − xj+1 ). The
complement costs at most a(tj+1 , xj+1 ). On the exceptional event, the global linear-growth
bound in (8.8) and the Gaussian tail estimate bound the cost by

                                Cx (1 + s(tj+1 )) exp{−cx 2j/2 /τ }.

Concavity and χ(1) = 0 give s(tj+1 ) ≤ 2j+1 s(t). The remaining term a(tj , xj ) tends to zero
by the uniform error εtj . Summing the rapidly decreasing tail series proves

                             sup a(t, h) ≤ Cx s(t) exp{−cx /(1 − t)}
                             h≥x

for t close to 1. Since χ(t) = t1 γ(v) dv ≥ (1 − t)γ(t), the drift γ(t)a(t, h) is bounded
                                    R

uniformly near time 1 on every compact interval contained in (0, ∞); explicitly it is at most
Cx (1 − t)−1 exp{−cx /(1 − t)} for h ≥ x.
    Start at a fixed late time, stop on leaving such an interval, and set the drift to zero
after exit. The resulting diffusion has bounded adapted drift up to time 1. The exponential
martingale in Girsanov’s theorem is integrable because that drift is bounded; its law is
equivalent to Brownian motion with its initial value. Its terminal law therefore has no atoms.
The original diffusion agrees with it on the event of no exit. Continuity of H implies that
every event {H1 = x} with x > 0 is covered by a countable union of these no-exit events,
using rational interval endpoints and rational starting times. Thus P(H1 = x) = 0.

Theorem 8.12 (The ordered limiting observables). The Gaussian observables in the problem
have all their stated ordered limits, at every u > 0 and s > 0, and are given by
                                                                      R
                                                                               
                 GJ (u) = P(0 < H1 ≤ u),            FJ (s) = P            ≤s R>0 .      (8.16)
                                                                     αc c
The force law has mean one and no atom at zero. These limiting laws are also the laws for
all Gaussian scale mixtures in the fixed-temperature comparison range. More explicitly, for
every 0 < δ < u and s > 0, the individual limits at each level of

                          lim lim lim GN,β,α (δ, u) = P(δ < H1 ≤ u),
                         α↓αc β→∞ N →∞

                                lim lim lim FN,β,α (s) = FJ (s)
                                α↓αc β→∞ N →∞

exist; the additional limit δ ↓ 0 in the first line gives GJ (u).

                                                   81
Proof. Thermodynamic and zero-temperature limits at fixed density. First take N → ∞
at fixed (β, α), using the inner empirical-marginal result proved earlier. At fixed UNSAT
density, Corollary 8.3 and (7.5) give the zero-temperature endpoint law, with no atoms. For
completeness, let hβ,α denote the inner limiting gap variable and put pβ,α = P(hβ,α < 0) and
µβ,α = E(hβ,α )− . Proposition 7.3 supplies uniform integrability of the negative first moment,
and the absence of an atom at zero gives

                            pβ,α −→ pα > 0,         µβ,α −→ µα > 0.

Positivity follows from αE(h∗α )2− = 2e(α) > 0. The inner limiting force CDF at s > 0 is
                                                              !
                                  1       µβ,α
                                     P −s      ≤ hβ,α < 0 .
                                pβ,α      pβ,α

Both limiting interval endpoints are atomless under the law of h∗α , so this expression converges
at every s > 0. Thus the full β → ∞ limit exists for all the required gap and force arguments,
and its conditioning mass is preserved before the density is changed. Uniqueness of every
subsequential endpoint law yields convergence along the full temperature limit.
    The critical-density limit and the final gap cutoff. Now approach αc from above. Propo-
sition 8.9 gives convergence of the physical endpoint gaps and the scaled residuals, and
Lemma 8.11 permits every positive cutoff, rather than just a selected continuity sequence.
    For gaps keep 0 < δ < u fixed throughout these three limits; the answer is P(δ < H1 ≤ u).
Only then let δ ↓ 0, which removes the contact atom and gives the first formula in (8.16).
For forces the conditional mean at density αn is cn /pn , which converges to αc c. Lemma 8.10
preserves the conditioning mass, and its cutoff argument loses no positive residual mass to
zero. Subtracting the noncontact mass and applying the positive-atom assertion gives the
second formula at every s > 0. Since ER = c and P(R > 0) = 1/αc , its mean is one. Its
conditional probability of {0} is zero; the uniform cutoff bound also explicitly prevents an
atom from appearing there in the limit of the physical conditional laws.
    Finally the Gaussian and mixture inner-limit observables agree at each fixed temperature
and density in a neighborhood above the common threshold, by Section 5. Equality of these
functions passes through exactly the same ordered limits. The limits u ↓ 0 and s ↓ 0, which
determine their exponents, are taken only in the subsequent sections.


9    Uniform control of the critical layer
Throughout this section, (χ, R) is the normalized critical optimizer, α = αc , c = ER > 0, and
Y = H1 . The inputs from Section 8 are the PDE and diffusion representation of Lemma 8.5,
the first-order conditions of Lemma 8.7, and the endpoint conclusions of Proposition 8.8 and

                                                Z 1

The increase support of γ accumulates at 1, and γ = 0 on an initial interval. At its interior
support points we have J = 0, J ′ ≤ 0, and (8.12), where J = P − I is the variational defect
defined in Section 8. All constants below may depend on this fixed optimizer and on a fixed
interior starting time; they do not depend on the distance to the endpoint. Constants with a
subscript may also depend on that parameter.
    Write τ = 1 − t. Whenever τ is the time argument, χ(τ ) means the original susceptibility
at physical time 1 − τ ; the same convention applies to the other deterministic time-dependent

                                               82
quantities. Thus χτ = γ ≥ 0 and χ(τ ) → 0 as τ ↓ 0; the susceptibility is concave in
this clock. The physical-time normalization remains χ(t = 0) = 1. In this clock write
S = {τ ∈ (0, 1) : 1 − τ ∈ supp dγ}. It has points arbitrarily close to zero. A support
gap means a component (τ1 , τ2 ) of its complement, on which χ is affine. When extending
estimates across gaps, we work below a fixed point of S; every intervening gap then has both
endpoints in S. Choosing this point sufficiently small puts both endpoints in the range of
any support-point estimate under consideration. Set

flow retains the Gaussian density at an early time; its Jacobian records how much the critical
drift compresses that law.
    Choose t0 > 0 in the interval on which γ = 0. Then χ(t0 ) = 1 and Ht0 has a strictly
positive Gaussian density p0 , independently of subsequent Brownian increments. Conditional
on those increments, the flow Ft taking Ht0 to Ht is increasing and convex: its first derivative
solves an equation with coefficient −dk ∈ [−d, 0], and its second derivative has nonnegative
forcing dmhh . If It = Ft−1 and Jt = It′ , then

  1 ≤ Jt ≤ χ(t)−1 ,        Jt is nonincreasing,          pt (h) = E[p0 (It (h))Jt (h)] ≤ C/χ(t).   (9.4)

9.3   A martingale bin comparison
We give the probability estimate needed to replace subpower control by a uniform bound.
Let (Zυ )0≤υ≤T be a nonnegative continuous martingale on a finite deterministic horizon, with
dZυ = συ dWυ and |συ | ≤ 1. Write u = T − υ for its remaining time. Suppose that, for each
fixed a > 0, there is ca > 0, independent of υ and the sample path, such that
                                 √
                           Z ≥ a u =⇒ |σ| ≥ ca > 0 (a > 0).                            (9.13)

We also assume that its law has a positive continuous density on (0, ∞) at each strictly
interior time 0 < υ < T . Continuity of its paths and absence of positive atomsRthen make each
moving-bin probability continuous for 0 < u < T . For r take variance time 0t s2 , remaining
                                                             √
time u = w, and |σ| = k. Condition (9.13) follows from m/ τ ≥ aD ≥ a, the upper barrier,
and a secant: the corresponding z is bounded above, and k is bounded below on that half-line.
No upper bound on D is needed here.
Lemma 9.4 (Bin comparison). Under these assumptions there exist 0 < u0 < T and finite
                                                                                        √
constants C, P , depending on the process and the constants ca , such that Mj (u) := P(j u ≤
                √
ZT −u ≤ (j + 1) u) satisfies

                       Mj (u) ≤ Cj P M1 (u),           j ≥ 2,      0 < u ≤ u0 .                   (9.14)

                                                  87
Proof. We first prove a conditional steering estimate; the martingale need not itself have the
Markov property. After any stopping time S, apply Brownian time change to the shifted
continuous martingale ZS+v − ZS in the filtration FS+v . Extending it with independent
Brownian increments after the terminal time if necessary, this gives ZS+v = ZS + WQv , where
Qv = SS+v σ 2 dt and W is Brownian with respect to a filtration containing FS at time zero.
       R

Its Brownian law thus holds conditionally on FS . All estimates below are uniform in that
initial information. Conditional spatial and time scaling permits us to set the remaining time
at S equal to one.                                     √
    Consider first a single step with time ratio ρ ∈ [ 2, 2], elapsed time L = 1 − ρ−1 , and
starting position x ∈ [1, 2]. Put
                          √                                                    3
             Lmin = 1 − 1/ 2,        κ = c1/2 ,        q∗ = κ2 Lmin /2,   yρ = √ .
                                                                              2 ρ

The terminal first bin is [ρ−1/2 , 2ρ−1/2 ]. Take the fixed spatial corridor (1/2, 3). Up to its
first exit, (9.13) gives κ2 ≤ Q̇ ≤ 1, since the remaining time is at most one. Let gx,ρ (q) move
linearly from x to yρ on [0, q∗ ], and stay at yρ on [q∗ , 1/2]. It lies in [1, 2]. On the Brownian
event
                                  sup |x + Wq − gx,ρ (q)| < 1/16,
                               0≤q≤1/2

no first corridor exit can occur before elapsed time L: at such an exit, Qv ≤ v ≤ L ≤ 1/2,
whereas the displayed event places the exit position strictly inside the corridor. This first-exit
argument establishes the lower clock bound without assuming it on the event being proved.
In particular QL ≥ κ2 L ≥ 2q∗ , so ZS+L is within 1/16 of yρ and lies in the terminal first
bin. The paths gx,ρ − x form a compact family of piecewise linear paths starting at zero.
Brownian motion has positive probability to stay in a fixed uniform neighborhood of each
such path; a finite cover by neighborhoods of radius 1/32 gives one positive lower bound for
the displayed event, independent of x and ρ. This elementary tube fact follows, for example,
by requiring finitely many Gaussian increments to lie in prescribed open intervals and the
intervening Brownian bridges√to stay in prescribed open strips.    √
    For any total ratio u′ /u ≥ 2, partition it into factors in [ 2, 2] and iterate the conditional
estimate. The number of steps is at most 1 + log2 (u′ /u), so the resulting probability is at
least c(u/u′ )P1 /2 for a fixed P1 . On the first step the starting interval may instead be
[1.5, J]: use corridor (1/2, J + 1) and the same piecewise linear path, obtaining a first-step
constant depending only on fixed J, with the same subsequent     √        power. There is also a
                                                        ′
uniform bound when starting at exactly 1.5 and u /u ∈ [1, 2]. In normalized units the event
supq≤Lmin |Wq | < 1/16 suffices, since [1.5 − 1/16, 1.5 + 1/16] lies inside every corresponding
terminal first bin. Here only QL ≤ L ≤ Lmin is used; no lower clock bound is necessary.
    The steering estimates now give a polynomial lower bound for reaching the first bin across
the scale ratios used below. We use that bound in an induction over decreasing dyadic slabs
of remaining time, comparing the other bins to the first and controlling distant starting bins
by Gaussian tails. On [u0 /2, u0 ], M1 has a positive minimum, so the assertion follows from
Mj ≤ 1. Suppose the assertion has been established on earlier slabs and let u lie in the next
slab. For j ≥ 2, set ua = min(j 2 u, u0 ) ≥ 2u. If ua = u0 , steering from its first bin gives
M1 (u) ≥ c(u/u0 )P1 /2 M1 (u0 ). Since j 2 u ≥ u0 , the trivial bound Mj (u) ≤ 1 proves the desired
inequality with power P1 and a fixed constant.
                                                                                          √
    In the uncapped case ua = j 2 u, condition at ua . Any path that starts below 1.5 ua and
                                                                      √

Proposition 9.6 (Two-sided endpoint mass estimates). There exist t1 < 1 and constants
0 < c1 < C1 < ∞ such that, for every t1 < t < 1,
                              √                         √
                  P(0 < R ≤ w) ≍ χ(t),         P(0 < Y ≤ τ ) ≍ χ(t).           (9.20)
Both comparisons use the same fixed constants c1 , C1 , after enlarging their range if necessary;
the time t need not be a support point.
Proof. For a lower bound, start the relevant martingale in the scaled interval [0.4, 0.6]. The
barriers, bounded D, and (9.16)–(9.19) put at least c0 χ mass in this interval. Its remaining
quadratic variation is at most the square of the corresponding scale. Brownian time change
shows that its displacement stays within 0.2 of that scale with a fixed positive probability: it
suffices that standard Brownian motion stay in [−0.2, 0.2] up to time one. Its endpoint is
then strictly positive and below the desired cutoff.
    For the force upper bound, paths starting in force bin j ≥ 2 must make a displacement
                   √                   √
of at least (j − 1) w to finish below w, and hence pay a Gaussian tail. Paths starting
                                                         √
in gap bin j ≥ 2 must make a displacement at least j τ to have R > 0, since that event
forces Y = 0. Sum the polynomial bin estimates against these Gaussian tails. The remaining
              √           √
region {r ≤ 2 w, X ≤ 2 τ } is in a fixed scaled spatial interval and has mass O(χ). For the
gap upper bound interchange the two martingales, using Y > 0 ⇒ R = 0. This proves both
statements.

9.5    Quantitative inverse-slope barriers
                                        √
For the rest of this section put V = − τ kh > 0. Since k decreases strictly from 1 to 0,
we may also regard V as a function of k ∈ (0, 1) at a fixed time. Subscripts z below mean
                         √

Lemma 9.7 (Inverse-slope bounds). At every interior critical time,
                               9                  6
                                 k(1 − k)3/2 ≤ V ≤ [k(1 − k)]3/4 .                            (9.21)
                               7                  5
Proof. We first establish the claim for a regularization that makes all endpoint comparisons
classical. In the approximants of (8.8), replace m by (λn + χn )an . Its terminal value is the
hinge, and it solves (9.1) with d = γn /(λn + χn ), 0 ≤ τ d ≤ 1. At fixed n this coefficient is
integrable. Approximate it in L1 by bounded coefficients satisfying the same inequality. Next
replace the initial hinge by its heat smoothing of variance e′ > 0 and replace the bounded
coefficient d(τ ) by d(τ )τ /(τ + e′ ). Put T = τ + e′ , and denote the replaced coefficient again by
d. Then D0 = T d ∈ [0, 1]. These operations converge on interior compact sets, first as e′ ↓ 0,
then in the coefficient approximation, then as n → ∞: the linear difference equation and
the uniform curvature and value bounds give local uniform convergence, and the semigroup
gradient estimates give convergence of spatial derivatives.
                                                     √           It is therefore enough to prove the
bounds uniformly for this regularization, with T in the definition of V .
    The slopes remain in [−1, 0] and are strictly monotone. Their endpoint values are −1 and
0. Write v = −kh , and let h = h(τ, k) be the inverse position. From the equation for k,

Its reverse diffusion representation shows that v vanishes uniformly at spatial infinity on a
compact time interval: starting positions tending to infinity remain there in probability, by

asserted endpoint behavior in inverse coordinates.

     At the heat-smoothed initial time, the inverse profile is V0 (k) = ϕ(Φ−1 (k)) and satisfies

times. One may apply the maximum principle on [δ, 1 − δ] and then let δ ↓ 0. The zeroth-
order coefficient in the linearized difference is bounded above: it involves (V + b)b′′ , which is
bounded above for the supersolution and, for the subsolution, on the only relevant region
V < b− . Multiplying the difference by a sufficiently decreasing exponential in time deals
with this bound. The uniform endpoint vanishing deals with the spatial boundary. Smooth
approximation handles measurable time coefficients. Passing through the regularizations
proves (9.21).

    Finally suppose (9.25) failed along a sequence to the endpoint. Extract a limit of

limiting density is uniformly bounded below there. The preceding strictly positive lower
bound contradicts failure. This proves (9.25).

terminal interval (t∗ , 1). On this interval J = J ′ = 0, and γ has a continuous version with

    Choose a support point t∗ beyond the time at which this determinant inequality holds
uniformly. Every support gap to the right of t∗ has both endpoints in the increase support:
the left endpoint lies at or after t∗ , and accumulation at 1 excludes a final gap. For any such
gap, stability gives ψ ≥ χ at those endpoints, whereas χ is affine on the gap. Strict concavity
consequently gives ψ > χ at every interior point. But then J ′ = αHb − s2 < 0 throughout
the gap, contradicting J = 0 at both endpoints. Thus there are no complementary intervals
to the right of t∗ . Closedness of the support proves that it contains (t∗ , 1).
    On this interval the variational conditions give J = J ′ = 0, Ek 2 = 1/α, and Ekl = cχ.
Apply Itô’s formula to k(t, Ht )2 :

                       d        1       2γ

                       dt       τ       χ
The denominator is strictly positive. Interior spatial regularity, the diffusion representation,
and dominated convergence make the quotient on the right of (9.26) continuous in t. It
supplies the continuous version of A0 , and hence of γ, and proves the asserted identity
everywhere on the terminal interval.


10     Selection of the critical similarity profile
The estimates of Section 9 leave a scalar quantity to identify: the limiting logarithmic
derivative of the susceptibility. We prove a rigidity statement for every limit obtained by
translating the critical layer in logarithmic time. Its quantitative part consists of finitely many
inequalities for one-dimensional functions. The analytic implications of those inequalities are
proved here; their finite verification and rounding-error bounds are given in Section 12. The

                                                   96
stationary scaling equations have their antecedents in [5, Section IX] and [10, Section V].
Here their selection is deduced from the entire limits of the critical problem constructed in
Sections 8–9.
    The coefficient reduction first confines A to [0, .41]. With this bound, stationary-reference
comparisons give curvature and density barriers for every entire critical limit. Using its
critical integral identities, signed tests then narrow the coefficient interval to [.285, .303],
where a strict contraction around the root forces A ≡ a∗ . Compactness finally transfers this
identification to the original critical layer. The opening table in Section 12 maps the finite
checks to these analytic steps.

10.1    Entire limits and their identities
We regard χ, γ, and m as functions of remaining time: their value at τ in this section is their
previous value at physical time t = 1 − τ . The density pt retains its physical-time index.
Write v = log τ , u = −v, and q = 1/2. Throughout this section the letters M, X, P refer to
rescaled quantities:
                                        √
                                m(τ, z τ )
                     M (v, z) =     √       ,      X(v, z) = z + M (v, z),
                                       τ
                                √
                                  τ           √              τγ
                     P (u, z) =       p1−τ (z τ ),      A=      .
                                χ(τ )                         χ

Thus P is a positive density divided by a mass scale and need not integrate to one. Set
k = −Mz , l = 1 − k, and V = −kz = Mzz > 0. Spatial derivatives in this section are
derivatives in z, except where inverse-slope coordinates are explicitly specified. Direct change
of variables gives

                               Mv = 21 Mzz + BMz + (A − q)M,
                                 B = qz + AM,                                               (10.1)
                                Pu = 12 Pzz − (BP )z + AP.

The opposite time directions are essential: M is parabolic in v, whereas P is parabolic in u.
The last term in the density equation comes from χu = −Aχ.

Lemma 10.1 (Compactness in logarithmic time). Every sequence un → ∞ has a subsequence
along which the translated rescaled profiles converge locally to a solution of (10.1) for all
u ∈ R. The limiting coefficient A is continuous and takes values in [0, 1]. The value barriers
(9.2), the inverse-curvature bounds (9.21), and the Gaussian bounds for k on the positive
half-line, l on the negative half-line, and V, Vz on both half-lines hold uniformly in entire time.
Moreover P > 0, P ≤ C exp(C|z|), and on every bounded spatial interval P has a positive
lower bound independent of entire time. The limits satisfy
                                                                Z
                     Z                Z                                 V 2 P dz
                          M XP dz =       klP dz = c,     A=       ZR               ,       (10.2)
                      R               R                        2        k 2 lP dz

where c = ER > 0 is the critical normalization constant.
                                                             √
Proof. The density estimate (9.16), together with p1−τ (0) τ /χ ≍ 1, bounds P above and
below on spatial compact sets. The same estimate bounds it globally by C exp(C|z| + o(1)z 2 ),
which is integrable against the Gaussian tail bounds already proved. The value and curvature
barriers bound the coefficients of (10.1) locally, uniformly after sufficiently late translation.

                                                97
Interior estimates for the localized heat equation, applied forward in v to M and forward
in u to P , give local bounds and equicontinuity for their spatial derivatives. Applying the
equations on a slightly smaller cylinder gives time equicontinuity. For precision, one may
first differentiate the localized heat-kernel formula in space: the initial C 1 bound controls
the drift term, and iteration gives the derivatives needed below. Bounded, initially merely
measurable, time coefficients suffice in this argument.
     On the full-support endpoint interval, (9.26) expresses A as a quotient of spatial integrals.
Its denominator, after division by χ, is bounded below uniformly. Indeed the value barriers
place an interior interval of slope values in a bounded spatial interval; inversion and (9.21) give
positive spatial width, and central positivity of P gives a positive integral of k 2 lP . Gaussian
tails and the density bound permit uniform truncation of both integrals. Consequently
the same compactness gives compactness of A itself. Pass first in the integral form of the
equations; the quotient then shows that the limiting A is continuous.
    The critical identities in physical units are E[rt Xt ] = cτ and E[kl] = cχ. They become the
first two integrals in (10.2). Their passage to the limit, and passage in the quotient, use the
value barriers, the negative-tail estimate (9.8), and the Gaussian domination just described.
The constants in all these arguments are uniform under translation, so the conclusions hold
on the whole time axis.

   We now use Q for the logarithmic spatial slope of P throughout this section. There is a
global, translation-uniform bound:

       Q = (log P )z ∈ [−C, 0],           Qu = 12 Qzz + (Q − B)Qz − (q − Ak)Q − AV.            (10.3)

Here nonincrease of P follows from the inverse-flow comparison in Section 9. To obtain the
lower bound for Q, compare two inverse positions corresponding to fixed rescaled positions
                                  √
z1 , z2 . Their displacement is O( w), so the ratio of the initial Gaussian densities tends to
one after truncating the inverse positions as in that comparison; the complement is negligible.
The inverse-Jacobian logarithmic ratio is bounded by C|z1 − z2 |, with C independent of the
chosen compact set. Hence in the limit | log P (z1 ) − log P (z2 )| ≤ C|z1 − z2 | for all z1 , z2 .
Differentiating the density equation gives (10.3).
     All subsequent integrations by parts can be made first with compact cutoffs. To justify
removal of the cutoffs also after differentiation, use cylinders of spatial radius (1 + |z|)−1
and time length (1 + |z|)−2 . Rescaling bounds the drift and its required spatial derivatives
there. Interior derivative estimates thus introduce only polynomial losses into the Gaussian
bounds for V and its derivatives and into the exponential bounds for P . These losses remain
integrable in every expression below.

Lemma 10.2 (Coefficient reduction). Every entire limit in Lemma 10.1 has 0 ≤ A ≤ 0.41.

Proof. We first show that the right side of (10.4) is strictly positive whenever 0.41 ≤ A ≤ 1.

        b = 2(0.6 − k),     E = −0.4tk ,           K = 12Ak − 5A − 1 − b2 + 0.8(1 − 2k).

                                      Z 1"                                     #

The boundary term at k = 1 vanishes by (9.21).
    The finite check checkA proves strict positivity of (10.5), uniformly for A ∈ [0.41, 1] and
k0 ∈ [0, 5/8]. Its input is only the two explicit bounds in (9.21). It partitions A and k,
subdivides the interval between the lower and upper allowed values of V , and integrates
cellwise lower bounds. For the boundary quadratic its maximum is at an endpoint when
b ≥ 0; when b < 0 it is decreasing for V ≥ 0. The two omitted endpoint cells each cost at
most 9 times their width, and on the first cell the boundary expression before its negation
is less than 0.001. A possible positive partial cell is subtracted rather than included in full.
These are exactly the corrections in checkA; Section 12 verifies the arithmetic.
     For completeness, positive cumulatives
