  f P = F (∞)P (∞) + F d(−P ) > 0; limits of truncated expressions give the same formula
when a tail value is infinite. The Gaussian/exponential bounds make the left endpoint product
zero and justify the limits. Strictness follows either from P (∞) > 0 or from the nonzero
positive measure d(−P ).
     Finally let L be the endpoint limsup of the original rescaled A. Choose translates realizing
L at time zero and take an entire limit. In that limit A(0) = L and A(u) ≤ L for all u.
If L ≥ 0.41, (10.4) makes Au (0) > 0, contradicting this maximum. Thus L < 0.41, which
implies the claimed, slightly weaker bound for every entire limit.

10.3    Stationary reference profiles
For a ∈ [0, 0.41], let Ma denote the entire solution of the first equation of (10.1) with A ≡ a
and the value barriers (9.2), in the inherited admissible class −1 ≤ Mz ≤ 0, Mzz > 0. This
solution exists and is unique. For existence, evolve the heat-smoothed hinge of variance one
with coefficient a/(1 + τ ) in the original m equation, and rescale by T = 1 + τ . The resulting
equation is autonomous in log T ; the heat and Mills-ratio barriers, together with (9.21), give
compactness of translates. For uniqueness, subtract two entire solutions. Their bounded
difference solves a linear equation whose zeroth-order coefficient is al2 − q ≤ a − q < 0 and

                                                   99
whose drift is qz + aM1 . Comparison from time v − T to v bounds its supremum by Ce−(q−a)T ;
let T → ∞. Time translation and uniqueness then imply stationarity. The same compactness
and uniqueness argument proves continuity of Ma in a on compact spatial sets, with its
needed spatial derivatives.
    The maximum principles used here and below are valid on the whole line. For bounded
differences, localize to [−R, R] and add εeCt (1 + z 2 ); bounded diffusivity and at most linear
drift allow C independent of R. Send R → ∞ and then ε ↓ 0. This also proves the
corresponding comparison for bounded ratios whenever their equation has bounded diffusivity
and at most linear drift.
    At a fixed reference, abbreviate p = q − a > 0 and W = Ma′′ . Its ODE and its derivative
give
                                                                                        tk

                                              2

logarithmic derivatives of k or l, and then their tail integrals. These formulas also bound the
tails of W by a polynomial times the same Gaussians.

mass outside large compact intervals uniformly small, and bounded one-dimensional Sobolev
norms give compactness on each such interval. Replace a minimizer by its absolute value;
the ODE and uniqueness for initial values make it strictly positive. The lowest eigenvalue
is simple, by the Wronskian identity for two positive square-integrable solutions. Smooth
compactly supported trials give upper semicontinuity of λ(a); compactness of minimizing
sequences gives lower semicontinuity. Thus λ is continuous.

10.4     Comparisons with the references
For the comparisons and integral tests, an input consists of a continuous coefficient A :
R → [0, 0.41] and a solution Mi (v, z) of the first equation in (10.1), defined for all v, z ∈ R.
We require 0 < ki = −Mi,z < 1, Vi = Mi,zz > 0, the scaled value barriers (9.2), and the
inverse-curvature bounds (9.21), uniformly in time. A density input also includes a positive
function Pi (u, z) whose logarithmic slope Qi = (log Pi )z is bounded on R2 , nonpositive, and
satisfies (10.3), with u = −v. The regularity is that of the entire limits above, so the equations
and comparisons hold classically on interior space–time cylinders.
    Every critical entire limit is an input by Lemmas 10.1 and 10.2. Every stationary Ma is
an input. The stationary density references used below, including every root in the certified
bracket, are density inputs by the tail construction and the bound Qa ≤ 0. The density
comparison class requires neither the full density equation in (10.1) nor the integral identities
(10.2). Indeed a stationary eigenreference with λ(a) ̸= a need not solve that density equation,
but its logarithmic derivative solves (10.3): spatial differentiation removes the constant
eigenvalue term. Consequently all the comparisons and the resulting Env enclosures apply
to such references. Later, the reference at 0.293650 enters only through direct functional
evaluation and interpolation. Converting a functional sign into a coefficient bound will
additionally require the homogeneous consequences of (10.2); a reference satisfies them at a
root. If a ≤ A ≤ b, then at the same spatial position
                                            Ma ≤ Mi ≤ Mb .                                     (10.7)
For example δ = Mi − Ma solves, in v, a linear equation with drift Bi , damping q − Ala ≥
q − b > 0, and source (A − a)Ma la ≥ 0. Its negative part vanishes by comparison from the
arbitrarily remote past. The upper comparison is identical with the inequality reversed.
   Curvatures must instead be compared at the same slope k. Fix a reference a, put

10.6    Inverse-coordinate functionals and their enclosures
The comparisons now give bounds for the fields at every entire time. We use them to
evaluate a signed integral whose sign determines whether A lies above or below a tested
value. Curvature is compared at equal slope, so we first express the other fields in that same
coordinate.
    The primitive reconstruction formulas are
                 Z k                              Z 1
                         j                              1−j
       M (k) =               dj,       X(k) =                 dj,        z(k) = X(k) − M (k).        (10.14)
                  0    V (j)                       k    V (j)

                                                        104
Indeed zk = −1/V , Mk = k/V , and Xk = −(1 − k)/V ; the zero constants at the appropriate
tails follow from the value barriers. These formulas fix the spatial origin, so no translation
parameter is suppressed when comparing curvatures at equal k.
    For a tested coefficient ξ and a nonnegative number c+ define
                                       Z 1
                                             P 2                        
                        Nξ (V, P ) =           V + c+ (M X − tk ) − 2ξktk dk.
                                        0    V
The critical identities have the following homogeneous form:
                  Z                                  Z                     Z
                                                             2
                        (M X − kl)P dz = 0,                V P dz = 2A             k 2 lP dz.     (10.15)
                    R                                  R                       R

They hold for each critical entire limit by (10.2), and for each stationary reference at a root
of λ(a) = a. For every input satisfying these identities, change of variables gives exactly
                                                            Z 1
                                                                  Pi
                                Nξ (Vi , Pi ) = 2(A − ξ)             ktk dk.
                                                              0   Vi
The value comparisons give Gaussian tails for M X and kl, and bounded Qi gives at most
exponential growth of Pi . The integrals are therefore finite; the one on the right is strictly
positive. Thus a sign enclosure for Nξ bounds A at every time; no assumption of stationarity
of the input is involved.
    The remaining construction turns the value, curvature and log-slope comparisons into
enclosures of this functional. All comparison bounds apply to inputs before the identities
(10.15) are imposed; only the conversion of a functional sign into a coefficient bound uses
those identities.
    Here we specify the mathematical meaning of the interval operations used in the certificate.
Its middle grid covers [κ, 1 − κ], where κ = 2−38 , and contains 1/2 as a cell boundary. A box
is an interval containing every value of a function throughout one closed grid cell. A sum
of boxes times cell widths encloses the corresponding integral. The operation partialsum
bounds a primitive ending anywhere in a cell: from the inclusive full-cell sum it subtracts
that cell’s box multiplied by the interval from zero to its width. The operation anchored
forms the analogous signed primitive from 1/2. Thus endpoints and partial cells are included,
not replaced by point sampling.
    The object Env applies (10.10) with

                rh = 1 + gea ,     rl = 1 − 1.1geb ,          Vh = rh Wa ,         Vl = rl Wb .

To bound M and X below, integrate 1/Vh in (10.14), writing it as the known a-reference
primitive plus the correction (1/rh − 1)/Wa . Use the b-reference and 1/rl for upper bounds.
Since g ≤ 0.41 and 0 ≤ e ≤ 0.13,

                            |1/rh − 1| ≤ 0.16g,            |1/rl − 1| ≤ 0.16g.

Hence the omitted correction in each primitive tail costs at most 0.16g times the corresponding
reference tail primitive. The coarser direct factor bound in adjusted is a second valid
enclosure, and intersection improves the result.
    Subtracting the primitive bounds encloses zi (k). A spatial range query in (10.13), with
this position box as input, then bounds Qi (zi (k)). Together with (10.10) and Qi ≤ 0 it yields
cellwise bounds sl , sh for
                                  d                 Qi (zi (k))
                                     log Pi (k) = −             .
                                  dk                  Vi (k)

Except in the final local comparison, normalize Pi (1/2) = 1 in inverse coordinates. The
primitive of these slope bounds is the logp enclosure. This normalization is made separately
at each fixed  time, after the evolution comparisons.   It preserves Qi and the homogeneous
identities (M X − kl)Pi = 0 and Vi2 Pi = 2A ki2 li Pi . No evolution equation for this
           R                          R             R

renormalized density is used in the functional tests.
    Conversely, the test of (10.12) needs possible slope values at a given spatial position.
The lower position bounds are replaced by their prefix minima and the upper bounds by
their suffix maxima, which preserves enclosure and makes inversion monotone. Invert the
resulting position intervals against the given spatial cell. At a clipped endpoint include the
corresponding entire k tail. The checked edge positions straddle the central region: the
positive edge is above 4, and the negative edge below −5.5. A spatial cell beyond an edge
uses that extreme slope cell and its tail. This is possiblek. Thus the density comparison
has no circular dependence on an assumed bound for Qi in its own residual test: checkH uses
only M and V enclosures to bound its input slopes.
    Several uniform tail bounds will be used in the integral tests. Equation (10.13) gives
Qi ≥ −0.4 for z ≤ −5.5: the central reference table checks Qb > −0.28 and Xb < 0.03 there,
and the reference S estimates continue the bounds outside the table. Also logp verifies

the absolute position of its grid edge in the tail under consideration. The checked bounds

10.9    Strict contraction and rigidity
Proposition 10.4 (Rigidity of the logarithmic-time limit). Let (Mi , Pi , A) be a density input
as defined in Subsection 10.4, and suppose that the two identities (10.15) hold at every entire
time. Then A ≡ a∗ . In particular every entire limit of the critical rescaled profiles has this
coefficient, and
                                 τ γ(τ )             log χ(τ )
                           lim           = a∗ , lim            = a∗ .
                            τ ↓0 χ(τ )           τ ↓0 log τ

The root of λ(a) = a in the certified interval is unique.

Proof. Let d = sup |A − a∗ | < 0.01. If d = 0 there is nothing to prove; assume d > 0.
Interpolate from the root to the input as above, with c+ = 0.3 and ξ = a∗ . Normalize the
two densities at the same spatial position zc = z∗ (1/2) to have value one. This normalization
preserves both identities (10.15). Unlike the previous normalization, it does not require the
input density at its own median slope to be one.
    Our target is |A(v) − a∗ | ≤ cloc d at every entire time, with cloc < 1. We obtain it by
bounding the change in the signed functional from its zero value at the root and dividing
by the positive denominator in the identity for Na∗ . The estimates below control that one
quotient, including its endpoint tails, by a constant times d.
   Write W = W∗ . Equation (10.17) implies

            |ϵ|             L
                ≤ Em :=           ,        |(Vi /W )−σ − 1| ≤ σdEm ,       Em < 3.1.
             d          1 − 0.01L
For the second inequality, if Vi /W < 1, convexity of t 7→ (Vi /W )−t puts its value below the
chord on [0, 1]; if Vi /W ≥ 1, use 1 − e−x ≤ x and the logarithmic bound. Integrating Em /W
with weights k and l in (10.14) gives bounds LM , LX for primitive changes divided by σd.
Their sum Lz bounds the position change:

                                   |zi (k) − z∗ (k)| ≤ dLz (k).

The code arrays lm, lx, lz enclose these functions; the global bounds 3.11M∗ and 3.11X∗
give convenient caps and tail allowances.
    The logarithmic density difference splits as

                            π(k) = log Pi (z∗ (k)) − log P∗ (z∗ (k))
                                    + log Pi (zi (k)) − log Pi (z∗ (k)).

The first term is zero at k = 1/2 and has k-derivative bounded by 2d/W , by (10.18). The
absolute value of the second is bounded by d times shifts: use the position interval z∗ ± dLz
and (10.18) to bound Qi there by a range query for Q∗ plus 2d. Hence

                             |π(k)|

                               d
These estimates enclose the interpolated v, p, M, X on each of the four parameter subintervals
used by localcheck.

                                               |Na∗ (Vi , Pi )|
                              |A(v) − a∗ | =                    ≤ cloc d.
                                                    Di
Taking the supremum yields d ≤ cloc d, contradicting d > 0. Thus every such density input
has A ≡ a∗ . In particular this holds for every critical entire limit. Compactness now implies
convergence of the original A to a∗ : otherwise a sequence with a fixed nonzero deviation
would produce an entire limit with that deviation at time zero. Finally d log χ/d log τ = A;
integrate between a fixed positive τ0 and τ , divide by log τ , and use the convergence of A to
obtain the logarithmic susceptibility limit.
    A stationary reference at any other root in the certified interval is a density input: its
value and curvature bounds were proved above, and its positive eigenfunction has bounded
nonpositive logarithmic slope satisfying (10.3). The stationary integral identities are exactly
(10.15). The implication just proved therefore forces its constant coefficient to equal a∗ . This
proves uniqueness in the interval without assuming that the other reference arises as a critical
translation limit.


11     The cumulative exponents
We now deduce the cumulative exponents from the selected susceptibility slope. This completes
the main theorem using the finite inequalities invoked in Section 10; their verification, including
rounding and exterior error bounds, follows in Section 12.

Completion of Theorem 1.1. Sections 5 and 6 establish the common threshold and mixture
comparison. Theorem 8.12 identifies the limits in the prescribed order (1.4), followed by
removal of the gap cutoff, using a nonnegative terminal gap Y = H1 and a nonnegative
terminal force variable R on the common Brownian space. If c = ER, then

12     Verification of the finite certificate
The computer-assisted part of the argument has three logically distinct components: integer
certificates for the stationary ODEs, analytic estimates that turn small residuals into errors
relative to the exact profiles, and interval bounds for the remaining integrals. This section
describes the interface between these components. In particular, a successful floating-point
calculation alone is not the assertion being used: every computed box must contain the
corresponding exact mathematical quantity. We first certify the reference profiles and
eigenfunction logarithmic derivatives using integer residuals and analytic error estimates; we

                                                 113
then enclose the barrier and integral tests by interval arithmetic and record the complete
execution.
   Throughout this section q = 1/2, 0 ≤ a ≤ .41, p = q − a ≥ .09, and
             B(z) = qz + aMa (z),         k = −Ma′ ,    l = 1 − k,       X = z + Ma .
The existence, uniqueness, and monotonicity of Ma , its ODE and tail bounds (10.6), and the
confining ground-state construction of Pa are analytic inputs from Section 10. All decimal
constants in inequalities below denote terminating decimals, hence rational numbers. Machine
padding constants are discussed separately in Subsection 12.4.
    The table maps the finite checks to their uses in Section 10. The enclosure proofs and
recorded execution below establish the finite inequalities; the comparison principles and
exterior estimates of that section supply their analytic consequences. The tail estimates used
with each line have been established in Section 10; a finite grid by itself would not justify any
statement over an infinite spatial domain.
   Routine                 Role in the analytic argument
   checkA                  Positivity of the cumulative lower bound (10.5), excluding an
                           entire limit with A ≥ .41.
   checkV                  Central strict inequalities (10.11), which validate the curvature
                           barriers (10.10).
   Env.checkH              Central positive damping and source bounds for (10.12), hence
                           the log-slope comparison (10.13), and the density bounds needed
                           for integration tails.
   Env.coarse              Signs of the left or right signed cumulatives for c+ = 0, with a
                           10−7 allowance for the adverse tail.
   nonlineartest           Signs of the full calibrated functional at c+ = .3, using geometric
                           interpolation and (10.16), with a 10−6 allowance.
   localcheck              The local barriers (10.17), (10.18), a positive denominator, and
                           the strict contraction coefficient, with a 2 · 10−6 allowance.
   certified               Integer residual and shooting tests, including the opposite signs
                           of λ(a) − a at the final two parameter values.

12.3    Inward shooting and eigenvalue certification
The shooting routine begins at x = 20. The value tailinit is an integer multiple of D−1
within D−1 of the exact rational number T (20). The routine revpoly expresses the stationary
polynomial in the reversed cell coordinate. In qcoefs, the resulting coefficients represent 2βe
with denominator ADD, and ke with denominator D. The inward cell step is −1/100; this
explains the sign in qint’s recurrence.
   For the completed degree-18 Riccati trial S, e the integer residual test is again a sum of
absolute coefficients on 0 ≤ s ≤ 1. It proves a residual smaller than 10−20 in the spatial
Riccati equation with the approximate β, e k.
                                            e The trial is exactly continuous between cells.
No numerical differentiation is used.
   Let ES = S − S. e With λ equal to the trial value,

                      ES′ = (2β − 2Se − ES )ES + fS ,       |fS | < 3 · 10−19 ,             (12.9)

where the source bound includes the residual and both stationary-profile errors from
Lemma 12.1. When λ differs from the middle trial value by at most 10−9 , add 2 · 10−9 to
this source bound.
    Under the provisional condition |ES | ≤ .001, each integer gs is a lower bound for the drift
in (12.9), with denominator ADD. The subtraction .002 in its definition covers |ES | and the
much smaller profile error. The tests on gs imply:

   1. every inward integrating factor, including factors with endpoints inside cells, is at most
      e2 ;

   2. the integrating factor from 20 to 0 is smaller than e−20 ;

   3. the factor from 20 to any x < 12.02 is smaller than e−6 .

For the last assertion, the cells indexed from 1203 upward have total integral greater than
8, while the sum of all negative cell contributions is greater than −2. This also covers the
possible partial cell.
    The initial error at 20, including variation of T (20) over the eigenvalue window, is less
than 2.41 · 10−6 . Variation of constants gives the following deliberately loose enclosures:

       |ES | < 2.9 · 10−5   (0 ≤ x ≤ 20),      |ES | < 4.5 · 10−7   (0 ≤ x < 12.02).       (12.10)

For example, the global estimate is bounded by e2 [2.41 · 10−6 + 20(2 · 10−9 + 3 · 10−19 )]; the
central estimate replaces the first factor e2 on the initial error by e−6 . Both are strictly below
the provisional .001, which closes the bootstrap and proves that the positive branch extends
to the center. For an endpoint trial compared at its own eigenvalue, the error at zero is
bounded instead by
                         (2.41 · 10−6 )e−20 + 20e2 (3 · 10−19 ) < 10−10 .

    The matching condition is S− (0) + S+ (0) = 0, because derivatives in z acquire opposite
signs on the two half-lines. At the lower and upper shooting values the integer tests give,
respectively, a trial sum smaller than −10−9 and greater than 10−9 . The total error of the two
halves is less than 2 · 10−10 , so these signs also hold for the true shoots. Continuity therefore
supplies a match. It produces a positive square-integrable eigenfunction after conjugation,
and consequently the lowest eigenvalue. We have proved

12.4    Floating-point enclosures
The interval stage uses IEEE binary64 round-to-nearest arithmetic, including square root.
Integer polynomial residual tests precede every tabulation. Let u = 2−53 be unit roundoff.
Away from underflow each elementary operation has relative error at most u; at underflow an
absolute error of one subnormal unit suffices. The functions down and up subtract or add

                                          (|x| + 10−280 )2−43

to a rounded endpoint. This is more than one thousand unit roundoffs in relative scale, and
its absolute component is much larger than a subnormal unit. Rounding in forming and
applying the padding consumes less than this allowance. Therefore the operations of class
I, including the four-product rule for multiplication, reciprocal on intervals of fixed sign,
squaring, and square root, enclose the exact operations. Assertions exclude division by a
zero-containing interval, invalid square roots, overflow, and invalid floating-point operations.
    The class constructor itself does not enlarge a literal. Integer and dyadic inputs are exact.
A terminating decimal that defines a mathematical barrier or functional is consequently
entered by rat(n,d), rather than by an unpadded decimal literal. The other non-dyadic
literals in the code are allowances or coarse caps with strict slack; they may be interpreted as
their binary64 values. In particular the caps .1431 and .1301 in adjusted are strictly weaker
than the analytic bounds they represent. Integer-coefficient conversion and node evaluation
require the separate allowance below.
    No library exponential or logarithm is used for interval evaluation. For |x| ≤ 100, iexp
evaluates a degree-20 Taylor polynomial at x/256, adds the interval remainder [−2−80 , 2−80 ],
and squares eight times. The remainder bound follows from e.4 .421 /21! < 2−80 . For 1/8 ≤
x ≤ 8, ilog takes three interval square roots and uses
                                                      !
                             t3         t21                                          x1/8 − 1
               log x = 16 t + + · · · +                   + remainder,          t=            .
                             3          21                                           x1/8 + 1

Here |t| < .14 and 16|t|23 /[23(1 − t2 )] < 2−60 , the remainder interval used by the code. All
polynomial operations in these two routines are interval operations.
   For summation of at most n real terms, the usual induction on rounded addition gives

                                n               n                    n
                               X              X              nu X
                              fl         yj −         yj ≤              |yj |
                                   j=1          j=1
                                                             1 − nu j=1

up to the negligible absolute underflow contribution. The quadrature arrays have n < 2 · 105 ,
so the coefficient is less than 3 · 10−11 . The routine sums uses the much larger allowance

                                                      118
10−9 times the accumulated absolute sum, plus tiny. This also covers rounding in that
accumulated sum and in the final correction. The shorter summation in checkA has at
most 4096 terms of absolute value below 9; its error after division by 4096 is less than
γ4096 9 < 4.1 · 10−12 . Its remaining scalar arithmetic costs less than 10−13 , since all terms
have magnitude less than 10. The subtraction of 2 · 10−9 therefore covers both, including the
unpadded bound arithmetic after the summation.
    The last scalar addition or division in the nonlinear and local tests is also unpadded.
The analytic allowances retain sufficient slack to cover it. In the recorded run all nonlinear
returned values have magnitude below .00204, so the final addition of the signed 10−6 changes
its exact value by less than 10−18 ; the two substantive errors total less than 6 · 10−7 , leaving
nearly 4 · 10−7 unused. For the local test, an additional diagnostic evaluation of the unchanged
function gave

        O = out.hi = .7290900413708653,                 D0 = den.lo = .7928198472818054.

With O < 1 and .7 < D0 < 1, rounding the final expression (O + 2 · 10−6 )/D0 costs less than
6 · 10−16 . The exterior and primitive-tail estimates cost less than 1.3 · 10−6 in the numerator,
leaving .7 · 10−6 unused there. Small rounding in the positive scalar calculation of terror
is absorbed in the same slack. Thus the returned local number is an upper bound for the
exact contraction coefficient, and the returned signed nonlinear numbers retain the required
mathematical signs.

12.5    From polynomial tables to spatial and inverse boxes
Each step of length .01 is sampled at the 50 fractions 0, 1/50, . . . , 49/50. The resulting spacing
is 1/5000. The coefficients for X, k, and l are formed by exact integer operations from those
of M ; they are evaluated independently. Here is a quantitative bound for the conversion and
evaluation. CPython’s integer true division computes the correctly rounded ratio cn /D: it
forms an integer quotient with guard bits, records the discarded remainder, rounds ties to
even, and then converts the rounded integer and rescales exactly.1 Every nonzero ratio here
is at least 10−36 in magnitude, so it is normal and has relative conversion error at most u.
Both an ordinary sample j/50 and a reversed sample 1 − j/50 have absolute node error at
most 2u, and the exact and rounded nodes lie in [0, 1]. Writing C = |cn |/D, the node
                                                                                P

perturbation therefore costs at most 36uC. The 36 rounded operations in degree-18 Horner
evaluation cost at most γ36 (1 + u)C, where γn = nu/(1 − nu); coefficient conversion costs
at most uC. The total is less than 74uC < 8.22 · 10−15 C, and in particular smaller than
2 · 10−14 C. The node allowance used for each of M, X, k, l is

                                 3 · 10−14       |cn |/D + 3 · 10−18 .
                                             X
                                                                                            (12.12)
                                             n

It covers this evaluation error, Lemma 12.1, and the additions and subtractions that form
node bounds. The code checks that the coefficient sum is less than 30D. The separate Q
allowances contain the evaluation error and (12.10); the global node radius is 2.95 · 10−5 and
the central refinement uses 5 · 10−7 .
    Between nodes, monotonicity gives boxes for M, X, k, l. For Q = (log P )′ , zcells first
bootstraps |Q| ≤ 1: the trial nodes have magnitude below .951, and the Riccati equation
bounds |Q′ | < 30 on a fine cell under this provisional bound. Its length is 1/5000, so
enlargement by .006 is sufficient and is strictly inside the provisional bound. Twice applying
the derivative equation then sharpens the cell box. The cap Q ≤ 0 uses the analytic
  1
   The implementation and its rounding argument are in CPython 3.12.13, long_true_divide,
Objects/longobject.c.


                                                  119
maximum-principle argument for the exact eigenprofile; the cap is not an additional numerical
assumption.
   Inverse lookup must also include uncertainty in node values. Since k is decreasing, suffix
maxima of its node lower bounds remain valid lower bounds, and prefix minima of its
upper bounds remain valid upper bounds. The analogous operations for increasing l give its
monotone guaranteed bounds. A binary search through these outward-monotonized arrays
supplies outer nodes for the inverse position. The code uses k or l according to which is better
separated from 1; the two schemes are mathematically equivalent. The inverse curvature is
then evaluated through the exact identity

                                      W = 2pM l + Xk,

rather than by differentiating an inverse interpolant.
    The routines Rmq.query and kquery return minima and maxima over all cells between
two indices. For a range of length n, take the two blocks of length 2⌊log2 n⌋ abutting its
endpoints. Their union covers the range, and their individual extrema have been computed by
successive doubling. Thus the returned box is valid even when these blocks overlap. Spatial
coordinate bounds and index calculations are enlarged by 10−12 or 10−10 before multiplication
and flooring; these allowances are larger than the binary64 errors on the bounded coordinate
range. This padding also validates the scalar negative-edge test 2pxc > 2, even though its
code uses the rounded parameter af. If xf is the negation of the padded upper position
bound used in that test, then xf < xc − 0.99 · 10−12 . Since p ≥ 0.09 and xc < 20, replacing
the rounded parameter and product by their exact values costs less than 3 · 10−14 , whereas
the position padding leaves more than 1.7 · 10−13 in 2pxc . Thus a successful scalar comparison
implies the exact tail-rate inequality.
    The inversion of the envelope zi (k) in possiblek and localcheck uses the same outward
monotonicity principle. At a clipped edge it explicitly restores the missing k-tail; a point
outside the central range is never silently treated as an interior grid point.
    More explicitly, let Lj , Uj be the outward-monotonized bounds in inverse cell j. If an
admissible inverse value belongs to the queried spatial interval [z− , z+ ], necessarily Lj ≤ z+
and Uj ≥ z− . The binary searches give the first and last cells satisfying these necessary
inequalities. Clipping either index to the available grid and restoring k = 0 or k = 1 therefore
retains all admissible slopes, including those in the omitted tails. For the local curvature
query the retained cells obey Vi (k) ≤ 1.03W (k) by (10.17). If an admissible k is in an omitted
tail, k(1 − k) ≤ κ and (9.21) gives instead

                          Vi (k) ≤ 1.2κ3/4 = 1.2 2−28.5 < 3.2 · 10−9 .

Consequently the code’s bound 1.031 wi.hi + .0001 contains the curvature in both cases; the
clipped query cannot discard a larger tail curvature.

12.6    Integral enclosures and their logical use
The integration grid covers [κ, 1 − κ] with κ = 2−38 , and has 2048 subdivisions per dyadic
scale. It splits at 1/2 and all edges and widths are exactly representable binary numbers: the
finest required denominator is 249 . A box on a closed cell, multiplied by its width, encloses
its integral. The routine partialsum removes an arbitrary fraction of the endpoint cell by
subtracting its box multiplied by [0, width]. This is valid for either sign of the integrand.
The routine anchored integrates separately to the left and right of 1/2, avoiding unnecessary
cancellation.
    The opening table records the analytic uses of these enclosures.


                                              120
    Several implementation details are important for this correspondence. The ratio caps
in Env apply to the exact profiles by the analytic barriers; primitive corrections use (10.14),
including the omitted primitive tail. In nonlineartest, the intermediate pairs are defined by
geometric interpolation solely inside the functional. No assertion that they solve a parabolic
equation is made or needed. Their log slopes lie in the same envelope because these slopes
interpolate affinely. The two forms of the density variation—direct integration against π, and
integration by parts against its anchored derivative—each enclose the same quantity; taking
their interval intersection is therefore justified. The quantity terror bounds the missing
primitive terms in that integration by parts, rather than the full exterior integral. These
are distinct errors: nonlineartest’s 10−6 contains both, and localcheck’s 2 · 10−6 contains
their local analogues after division by d.
    These primitive-tail allowances require derivative bounds, which can be checked directly.
Put r = Vi /W and vσ = W rσ . Then

                                    ∂σ (vσ−1 ) = −(log r)vσ−1 .

In the local regime, | log r|/d ≤ L/(1 − .01L) < 3.1, so differentiating the positive integrals in
(10.14) gives |∂σ Mσ | ≤ 3.1dMσ and the identical estimate for Xσ . Relative to the reference
integrands, | log r|r−σ /d ≤ 3.1/.97 < 4, proving the primitive-tail coefficient 4 after division
by d. For the nonlinear refinement, 1/3.8 < r < 3.8 gives instead | log r|r−σ ≤ 3.8 log 3.8 < 6.
This proves its coefficient 6. The distinct finite-difference bound |r−σ − 1| < 2.8 < 3.01
justifies its primitive correction. These arguments use dominated differentiation with the
reference primitive as an integrable majorant; no derivative estimate is inferred merely from
a finite-difference estimate.
    The omitted endpoint cells of checkA admit particularly crude bounds. In the notation
of (10.5), |b| ≤ 1.2, b ≥ −.8, E = −.4tk , tk ≤ 1/4, and K ≥ −8.24. Since V < .5 and
6A2 − .16 > 0 for .41 ≤ A ≤ 1, its integral density satisfies

           2V 2 + KV − 2bE + (6A2 − .16)t2k /V ≥ −8.24 · .5 − .16 = −4.28 > −9.

This holds throughout the interval, hence on both omitted cells. At 0 ≤ k0 ≤ 1/4096, the
endpoint expression obeys
                                           3/2
            bV 2 + 2EV ≤ 1.2V 2 ≤ 1.728tk        ≤ 1.728/262144 < 6.6 · 10−6 < .001.

Thus its endpoint allowances also have analytic coverage.
    The driver follows the successive output intervals listed with the signed tests in Section 10:
eleven coarse updates from [0, 0.410], then thirteen nonlinear updates, give [0.285, 0.303].
Each printed checkpoint is the input interval for the checks about to run. In particular, the
last printed interval is [0.283, 0.304]; successful completion of its two sign tests proves the
final interval [0.285, 0.303]. The slight enlargement from 0.369 to 0.370 in the coarse sequence
keeps a valid coefficient enclosure. All sign tests include the error allowances described above.
Their analytic consequence is the input enclosure required by the local contraction, which
then forces A ≡ a∗ .

12.7    Reproduction and numerical output
The accompanying file certificate/original_certificate.py is the unaltered code
printed in the appendix. From the manuscript directory, run

       python3 certificate/original_certificate.py



                                                 121
with NumPy installed and assertions enabled; do not use -O. The certificate requires IEEE
binary64 arithmetic with round-to-nearest operations as described above. Its interval check-
points are printed to standard output. The accompanying reproducibility record identifies
the software versions, source hashes, and saved output of a complete run.
    The complete unchanged driver passed on October 4, 2026, under Python 3.12.13 and
NumPy 2.3.5, with assertions enabled, in 338.52 seconds. It printed all 24 checkpoints and
had empty standard error; the actual output is retained with the certificate. The separate
return-observer record from September 24, which leaves the source, function arguments and
results unchanged, gives

 checkA() = 0.002977541856187091 > 0,           localcheck() = 0.9196188060510444 < .95.

The displayed decimals are a returned lower bound and upper bound, respectively. A fresh
targeted evaluation of the unchanged local routine reproduces the second value and the
numerator and denominator reported in Subsection 12.4. The complete unchanged execution
and this diagnostic evaluation are recorded separately. Their interpretation as rigorous
bounds is supplied by the error analysis and comparison arguments above. The exact source
hash is
     7bd5dbd918d9a48fe4da62044e295239b74f7a1607e2643337ad4d073a85dc7f.

The integer seeds for the final two spectral tests are 2936535033 and 2936533047, with
denominator 1010 . Formula (12.11) therefore yields

                         .2936535023 < λ(.2936533) < .2936535043,
                         .2936533037 < λ(.2936535) < .2936533057.

In particular the first eigenvalue is strictly larger than its parameter and the second strictly
smaller. Continuity supplies

                                  .2936533 < a∗ < .2936535.

The contraction argument applies to every root in this interval and proves its uniqueness
there. Combined with the analytic identification of the critical exponents, these rational
inequalities give the exponent bounds stated in the conclusion.

    A     Executable certificate
    The following listing is the complete certificate distributed with the manuscript. Run it with
    Python 3 and NumPy, with assertions enabled; see Section 12 for the relation between the
    finite checks and the analytic estimates. An arrow at the start of a displayed line marks a
    continuation of the same source line.
1  import math, numpy as np
2  D=10**36
 3 HN=100 # step reciprocal
 4 L=24
 5 N=L*HN
 6 DEG=18
 7 AD=10**6 # a denominator
 8 LD=10**10
 9 # all stored coefficients are integers /D
10 def mstep(A, side, j, c0,c1, check=False):
11  # z0=side*j/HN, h=side/HN, q=.5, a=A/AD
12  c=[c0,c1]
13  # recurrence common h2 terms: 2(q-a-q*n)c_n -2q*(z0/h)*(n+1)c_{n+1}; nonlinear -2*a/h ...
14  # denominator HN2 AD D
15  def rhs(n):
16   val=0
17   if n<len(c): val+=(AD*(1-n)-2*A)*D*c[n]
18   if n+1<len(c): val-=j*(n+1)*AD*D*c[n+1]
19   conv=sum(c[i]*(n-i+1)*c[n-i+1] for i in range(max(0,n+2-len(c)),min(n+1,len(c))) )
20   val-=2*A*side*HN*conv
21   return val
22  den=HN**2*AD*D
23  for n in range(DEG-1):
24   c.append(rhs(n)//(den*(n+2)*(n+1)))
25  if check:
26   # equation residual in M_zz - rhs/h2 ; scale D2 AD
27   err=sum(abs(((c[n+2]*(n+2)*(n+1)*den) if n+2<len(c) else 0)-rhs(n)) for n in range(2*DEG+1))
28   assert err*10**23 < AD*D**2, (side,j,err/(AD*D**2))
29  return c
30 def mint(A,m0,k0,save=False):
31  data=[]; ends=[]
32  for side in [-1,1]:
33   arr=[]
34   c0=m0
35   # to ensure initial derivative exactly same with sides define k0 here as k0/HN scaled!
36   c1=-side*k0
37   for j in range(N):
38    c=mstep(A,side,j,c0,c1,save)
39    c0=sum(c);c1=sum(n*x for n,x in enumerate(c))
40    if save: arr.append(c)
41   ends.append(c0-(L*D if side<0 else 0));data.append(arr)
42  if save:
43   assert max(abs(x) for x in ends)*10**23 < D
44   return data
45  return np.array([x/D for x in ends])
46 LQ=20
47 def revpoly(c):
48  return [(-1)**j*sum(c[i]*math.comb(i,j) for i in range(j,len(c))) for j in range(len(c))]
49 def qcoefs(A,data):
50  out=[]



                                                    124
51   for side,arr in zip([-1,1],data):
52    barr=[];karr=[]
 53   for j,m in enumerate(arr[:LQ*HN]):
 54    mr=revpoly(m)
 55    # beta stored scaled AD D
 56    beta=[2*A*side*x for x in mr]
 57    beta[0]+=AD*D*(j+1)//HN;beta[1]-=AD*D//HN
 58    # physical derivative z: dx reversed -1/HN
 59    k=[side*HN*n*mr[n] for n in range(1,len(mr))]
 60    barr.append(beta);karr.append(k)
 61   out.append((barr,karr))
 62  return out
 63 from fractions import Fraction as Fr
 64 def tailinit(A,Lam,side):
 65  p=Fr(1,2)-(Fr(A,AD) if side<0 else 0)
 66  c=Fr(Lam,LD)/p-1
 67  assert Fr(-71,100)<c<Fr(81,100) and (side>0 or c>Fr(-1,100))
 68  d=c*(c-1)/(2*p);e=(2*c-3)*d/(2*p)
 69  val=c/LQ+d/LQ**3+e/LQ**5
 70  return val.numerator*D//val.denominator
 71 def qint(A,Lam,coefs,save=False):
 72  ends=[];out=[]
 73  for side,(barr,karr) in zip([-1,1],coefs):
 74   s0=tailinit(A,Lam,side); arr=[]
 75   # h negative; residual scaled LD AD D2
 76   for beta,k in zip(barr[::-1],karr[::-1]):
 77    c=[s0]
 78    def rhs(n):
 79     convb=sum(c[i]*beta[n-i] for i in range(max(0,n+1-len(beta)),min(n+1,len(c))))
 80     convs=sum(c[i]*c[n-i] for i in range(max(0,n+1-len(c)),min(n+1,len(c))))
 81     val=LD*(convb-AD*convs)
 82     if n<len(k): val-=2*LD*D*A*k[n]
 83     if n==0: val+=(LD-2*Lam)*AD*D**2
 84     return -val # sign h
 85    den=HN*LD*AD*D
 86    for n in range(DEG):
 87     c.append(rhs(n)//(den*(n+1)))
 88    s0=sum(c)
 89    if save:
 90     err=sum(abs(((c[n+1]*(n+1)*den) if n+1<len(c) else 0)-rhs(n)) for n in range(2*DEG+1))
 91     assert err*10**20 < LD*AD*D**2
 92     arr.append(c)
 93   ends.append(s0);out.append(arr[::-1])
 94  if save:return ends,out
 95  return sum(ends)/D

165 # float interval library; padding assumes IEEE

167 tiny=1e-280
168 def down(x):return x-(abs(x)+tiny)*2**-43
169 def up(x):return x+(abs(x)+tiny)*2**-43
170 class I:
171  def __init__(self,lo,hi=None):
172   self.lo=lo;self.hi=lo if hi is None else hi
173  def __add__(x,y):
174   y=iv(y);return I(down(x.lo+y.lo),up(x.hi+y.hi))
175  __radd__=__add__
176  def __neg__(x):return I(-x.hi,-x.lo)
177  def __sub__(x,y):return x+-iv(y)
178  def __rsub__(x,y):return iv(y)+-x
179  def __mul__(x,y):
180   y=iv(y)
181   a=x.lo*y.lo;b=x.lo*y.hi;c=x.hi*y.lo;d=x.hi*y.hi
182   return I(down(np.minimum(np.minimum(a,b),np.minimum(c,d))),up(np.maximum(np.maximum(a,b),np.maximum(c
       ,→ ,d))))
183  __rmul__=__mul__
184  def inv(x):
185   assert np.all(x.lo*x.hi>0)
186   return I(down(1/x.hi),up(1/x.lo))
187  def __truediv__(x,y):return x*iv(y).inv()
188  def __rtruediv__(x,y):return iv(y)*x.inv()
189  def sq(x):
190   a=x.lo*x.lo;b=x.hi*x.hi
191   return I(np.where((x.lo<=0)&(x.hi>=0),0,down(np.minimum(a,b))),up(np.maximum(a,b)))
192  def sqrt(x):


                                                     126
193   assert np.all(x.lo>=0)
194   return I(np.maximum(0,down(np.sqrt(x.lo))),up(np.sqrt(x.hi)))
195  def cap(x,lo,hi):
196   z=I(np.maximum(x.lo,lo),np.minimum(x.hi,hi));assert np.all(z.lo<=z.hi)
197   return z
198  def sel(x,s):return I(x.lo[s],x.hi[s])
199  def maxabs(x):return np.maximum(abs(x.lo),abs(x.hi))
200 def iv(x):return x if isinstance(x,I) else I(x)
201 def rat(n,d):return I(n)/d
202 def choose(cond,x,y):
203  x=iv(x);y=iv(y);return I(np.where(cond,x.lo,y.lo),np.where(cond,x.hi,y.hi))
204 # exp interval Taylor scale /256, require |x|<=100
205 def iexp(x):
206  x=iv(x);assert np.all(x.maxabs()<=100)
207  def fn(y,upper):
208   # Taylor point using interval class
209   w=I(y)/256; t=I(1.)
210   for i in range(20,0,-1):t=1+w*t/i
211   t=t+I(-2**-80,2**-80) # exp(.4) *.4^21/21! < 2^-80
212   for i in range(8):t=t.sq()
213   return t.hi if upper else t.lo
214  return I(fn(x.lo,False),fn(x.hi,True))
215 # log for args [1/8,8] via repeated sqrt and atanh
216 def ilog(x):
217  x=iv(x); assert np.all(x.lo>=.125) and np.all(x.hi<=8)
218  for _ in range(3):x=x.sqrt()
219  t=(x-1)/(x+1); ts=t.sq()
220  p=rat(1,21)
221  for i in range(19,0,-2):p=rat(1,i)+ts*p
222  return 16*t*p+I(-2**-60,2**-60) # |t|<.14

224 ZF=50 # fine per HN =.0002
225 ZN=HN*ZF
226 def polytab(intarr,reverse=False):
227  # table each cell at s=0,...,(ZF-1)/ZF; q reversed variable use 1-s
228  co=np.array([[float(v/D) for v in c] for c in intarr])
229  frac=np.arange(ZF)/ZF
230  if reverse:frac=1-frac
231  val=co[:,[-1]]
232  for j in range(co.shape[1]-2,-1,-1): val=val*frac+co[:,[j]]
233  # final endpoint omitted
234  return val.reshape(-1)
235 class Profile:
236  def __init__(self,A):
237   data,qdata,lam=certified(A)
238   self.A=A; self.a=rat(A,AD); self.af=A/AD
239   self.lam=rat(lam,LD)+I(-1.01e-9,1.01e-9) # use pads later
240   sides=[]
241   for side,arr,qs in zip([-1,1],data,qdata):
242    arr=arr[:LQ*HN]
243    mx=[];ks=[];ls=[]
244    for j,c in enumerate(arr):
245     xx=c.copy();xx[0]+=side*j*D//HN;xx[1]+=side*D//HN;mx.append(xx)
246     kk=[-side*HN*n*v for n,v in enumerate(c) if n>0];ks.append(kk)
247     ll=[-v for v in kk];ll[0]+=D;ls.append(ll)
248     # error bound for polytab rounding using sumabs
249     for coeff in [c,xx,kk,ll]:
250      # absolute eval error <=2e-14 sumabs; want 1e-14 when small; adaptive use? store separately
251      assert sum(abs(x) for x in coeff)<30*D
252    sides.append([polytab(arr),polytab(mx),polytab(ks),polytab(ls),side*polytab(qs,True)])
253   # join z indices -(20-1/ZN)..(20-1/ZN)
254   self.offset=LQ*ZN-1
255   self.M,self.X,self.k,self.l,self.Q=[np.concatenate([x[:0:-1],y]) for x,y in zip(*sides)]
256   # adaptive rounding error: for M,X,k,l near small sumabs maybe bound globally tighter by cell checking
257   # compute arrays of err coefficients for each
258   errsides=[]
259   for side,arr in zip([-1,1],data):
260    lists=[[] for _ in range(4)]
261    for j,c in enumerate(arr[:LQ*HN]):
262     xx=c.copy();xx[0]+=side*j*D//HN;xx[1]+=side*D//HN
263     kk=[-side*HN*n*v for n,v in enumerate(c) if n>0]
264     ll=[-v for v in kk];ll[0]+=D


                                                      127
265      for li,coef in zip(lists,[c,xx,kk,ll]):
266       li.append(sum(abs(x) for x in coef)/D*3e-14+3e-18)
267     errsides.append([np.repeat(li,ZF) for li in lists])
268    self.err=[np.concatenate([x[:0:-1],y]) for x,y in zip(*errsides)]
269    # monotone search approximate k and l enforce using certified thresholds cumulative
270    # exact k decreasing. guaranteed lower k and upper l prefix if node bound; take suffix max lower k?
271    self.klo=np.maximum.accumulate((self.k-self.err[2])[::-1])[::-1] # suffix nodes prove lower
272    self.khi=np.minimum.accumulate(self.k+self.err[2]) # prefix upper
273    self.llo=np.maximum.accumulate(self.l-self.err[3]) # prefix lower (l inc)
274    self.lhi=np.minimum.accumulate((self.l+self.err[3])[::-1])[::-1]
275   def at(self,name,idx):
276    vals=getattr(self,name)[idx]
277    er=(2.95e-5 if name==’Q’ else self.err[[’M’,’X’,’k’,’l’].index(name)][idx])
278    return I(vals)-0+I(-er,er) # padded
279   def inverse_indices(self,k,l): # intervals arrays, find z interval outer nodes
280    use=k.hi<=.6
281    # lower idx: want node k_lower >= khi, choose last such; equivalently search on -klo
282    l1=np.searchsorted(-self.klo,-k.hi,side=’right’)-1
283    u1=np.searchsorted(-self.khi,-k.lo,side=’left’)
284    # l increasing: lower node upper l <= llo
285    l2=np.searchsorted(self.lhi,l.lo,side=’right’)-1
286    u2=np.searchsorted(self.llo,l.hi,side=’left’)
287    low=np.where(use,l1,l2);hi=np.where(use,u1,u2)
288    assert np.all(low>=0) and np.all(hi<len(self.k))
289    return low,hi
290   def inverse(self,k,l):
291    low,hi=self.inverse_indices(k,l)
292    z=I((low-self.offset)/ZN-1e-12,(hi-self.offset)/ZN+1e-12)
293    M=I(self.at(’M’,hi).lo,self.at(’M’,low).hi).cap(0,100)
294    X=I(self.at(’X’,low).lo,self.at(’X’,hi).hi).cap(0,100)
295    W=2*(.5-self.a)*M*l+X*k
296    return z,M,X,W

298   from functools import lru_cache
299   profiles=lru_cache(maxsize=6)(Profile)

302 CUT=38
303 def grid():
304  ed=np.concatenate([(1+np.arange(MGRID)/MGRID)*2.**(-i) for i in range(CUT,1,-1)]+[np.array([.5])])
305  ed=np.concatenate([ed,1-ed[-2::-1]]) # exact dyadic? smallest subdivisions require CUT+11=49 <53 yes
306  w=np.diff(ed); assert len(w)%2==0
307  return I(ed[:-1],ed[1:]),I(1-ed[1:],1-ed[:-1]),w,len(w)//2
308 kg,lg,width,mid=grid()
309 def sums(x,rev=False): # intervals of weighted cumulative over cells inclusive full endpoint
310  v=x*I(width)
311  def side(a,sgn):
312   if rev:a=a[::-1]
313   cs=np.cumsum(a)
314   err=np.cumsum(abs(a))*1e-9+tiny # n<1e6
315   out=cs+sgn*err
316   return out[::-1] if rev else out
317  return I(side(v.lo,-1),side(v.hi,1))
318 def partialsum(x,rev=False): # integral to any point in current cell from relevant tail (within grid)
319  v=x # allow either sign
320  full=sums(v,rev)
321  # remove optional part
322  amt=x*I(0,width)
323  return full-amt
324 def total(x):
325  sm=sums(x);return I(sm.lo[-1],sm.hi[-1])
326 def anchored(x): # integral from .5 to any cell; avoid total cancellation
327  left=choose(np.arange(mid*2)<mid,x,0)
328  right=choose(np.arange(mid*2)>=mid,x,0)
329  return choose(np.arange(mid*2)<mid,-partialsum(left,True),partialsum(right))
330 # RMQ for cell bounds on z table adjacent nodes
331 class Rmq:
332  def __init__(self,g,values):
333   self.g=g;self.los=[values.lo];self.his=[values.hi]
334   while 2**len(self.los)<=len(values.lo):
335    step=2**(len(self.los)-1)
336    self.los.append(np.minimum(self.los[-1][:-step],self.los[-1][step:]))


                                                       128
337    self.his.append(np.maximum(self.his[-1][:-step],self.his[-1][step:]))
338  def query(self,z):
339   low=np.floor((z.lo-1e-10)*ZN).astype(int)+self.g.offset
340   high=np.floor((z.hi+1e-10)*ZN).astype(int)+self.g.offset
341   assert np.all(low>=0) and np.all(high<len(self.los[0]))
342   lens=high-low+1
343   olo=np.empty_like(z.lo);ohi=np.empty_like(z.lo)
344   for j,(los,his) in enumerate(zip(self.los,self.his)):
345    sel=(lens>=2**j)&(lens<2**(j+1)); u=low[sel];v=high[sel]-(2**j-1)
346    olo[sel]=np.minimum(los[u],los[v]);ohi[sel]=np.maximum(his[u],his[v])
347   return I(olo,ohi)
348 def zcells(g):
349  idx=np.arange(len(g.M)-1)
350  def monot(name,inc):
351   vals=g.at(name,np.arange(len(g.M)))
352   return I(vals.lo[:-1] if inc else vals.lo[1:],vals.hi[1:] if inc else vals.hi[:-1])
353  M=monot(’M’,False).cap(0,30);X=monot(’X’,True).cap(0,30)
354  k=monot(’k’,False).cap(0,1);l=monot(’l’,True).cap(0,1)
355  z=I((idx-g.offset)/ZN-1e-12,(idx+1-g.offset)/ZN+1e-12)
356  beta=.5*z+g.a*M
357  # bootstrap derivative bound: start Q node error, assume |Q|<=1 within length => |Q’|<25, enlarge,
       ,→ refine
358  qnodes=g.at(’Q’,idx)
359  # refine error central (cert proof)
360  qnodes=qnodes.cap(g.Q[idx]-np.where(abs(z.lo)<12.01,5e-7,3e-5),g.Q[idx]+np.where(abs(z.lo)<12.01,5e
       ,→ -7,3e-5))
361  Q=qnodes+I(-.006,.006)
362  for _ in range(2):
363   qp=2*beta*Q-Q.sq()+2*(.5-g.a*k-g.lam)
364   Q=(qnodes+I(-1,1)*I(qp.maxabs())/ZN).cap(-1,0)
365  qp=2*beta*Q-Q.sq()+2*(.5-g.a*k-g.lam)
366  V=2*(.5-g.a)*M*l+X*k
367  return locals()
368 def eloc_z(z,cp): # .25 power
369  t=2-z
370  t=I(np.maximum(t.lo,0),np.maximum(t.hi,0))
371  den=1+t.sq()/2
372  e=cp/2/den.sqrt().sqrt()
373  ep=e*t/(4*den)
374  epp=e*(5*t.sq()/(16*den.sq())-1/(4*den))
375  epp=I(np.where(z.hi>=2,np.minimum(0,epp.lo),epp.lo),np.where(z.hi>=2,np.maximum(0,epp.hi),epp.hi))
376  return e,ep,epp
377 def checkV(g,gapA,upper,cp):
378  d=zcells(g);a=g.a
379  p=.5-g.af
380  sel=(d[’z’].lo<6)&(d[’z’].hi> -math.sqrt(13/p)-.01)
381  z,M,k,l,W,B=[d[x].sel(sel) for x in [’z’,’M’,’k’,’l’,’V’,’beta’]]
382  t=k*l
383  T=2*(.5-a)*M*l/W-2*k+2*a*(t/W).sq()
384  if g.A==0: assert np.min(T.lo)>-1.12 and np.max(T.hi)<.13
385  e,ep,epp=eloc_z(z,cp)
386  fac=rat(1,1) if upper else -rat(11,10)
387  R=1+fac*e*gapA
388  Ar=(a+I(0,1)*gapA) if upper else (a-I(0,1)*gapA)
389  core=.5*epp+(-B+a*t/W)*ep
390  rhs=R.sq()*core+Ar*t/W*ep+R*(-(R+1)*(.5+a*T)*e+I(np.maximum(T.lo,0),np.maximum(T.hi,0))/(fac if upper
       ,→ else -fac))
391  core0=fac*gapA*core-(.5+a*T)*R
392  return np.max(rhs.hi),np.max(core0.hi)
393 CP=rat(26,100); ERR=rat(12,100)
394 def tail_mx_bounds(g): # cut primitives station first/last cell from inverse already include true cutoff
395  z,m,x,v=g
396  return m.hi[0],x.hi[-1]
397 class Env:
398  def __init__(self,A,B,check=True):
399   self.a=profiles(A);self.b=profiles(B); gap=self.gap=self.b.a-self.a.a
400   if check:
401    for g,u in [(self.a,1),(self.b,0)]:
402     rr,co=checkV(g,gap,u,CP); assert rr<0 and co<-.02,(g.A,rr,co)
403   self.va0=self.a.inverse(kg,lg); self.vb0=self.b.inverse(kg,lg)
404   za,ma,xa,wa=self.va0; zb,mb,xb,wb=self.vb0
405   rh=1+gap*eloc_z(za,CP)[0]; rl=1-rat(11,10)*gap*eloc_z(zb,CP)[0]


                                                     129
406    self.vhi=rh*wa; self.vlo=rl*wb
407    # envelopes primitive correction
408    def adjusted(box,w,R,weight,rev):
409     corr=1/R-1
410     tail=I(-.16,.16)*gap*(box.sel(-1 if rev else 0))
411     out=box+tail+partialsum(weight/w*corr,rev)
412     direct=box/(1+I(-.1431,.1301)*gap)
413     return out.cap(direct.lo,direct.hi)
414    ml=adjusted(ma,wa,rh,kg,False);xl=adjusted(xa,wa,rh,lg,True)
415    mh=adjusted(mb,wb,rl,kg,False);xh=adjusted(xb,wb,rl,lg,True)
416    self.m=I(ml.lo,mh.hi);self.x=I(xl.lo,xh.hi)
417    self.z=self.x-self.m
418    # Q combo cell rmq
419    da=zcells(self.a);db=zcells(self.b)
420    delta=(db[’M’]-da[’M’]).cap(0,30) # may fail over tiny equal? intervals overlap
421    upperq=da[’Q’]+self.a.a*delta
422    lowerq=db[’Q’]-self.b.a*delta
423    qhi=Rmq(self.a,upperq).query(self.z)+ERR*gap
424    qlo=Rmq(self.b,lowerq).query(self.z)-ERR*gap
425    qhi=I(np.minimum(0,qhi.lo),np.minimum(0,qhi.hi))
426    self.sl=I(np.maximum(0,(-qhi/self.vhi).lo)); self.sh=I((-qlo/self.vlo).hi)
427    # actual slope between sl.lo and sh.hi (qlo.lo negative)
428    self.slope=I(self.sl.lo,self.sh.hi)
429    self.logp=anchored(self.slope)
430    if check:self.checkH(da,db,delta)
431   def possiblek(self,z): # central away grid tails
432    # high k where zhi >= z.lo; low k where zlo<= z.hi
433    low=np.minimum.accumulate(self.z.lo) # enforce decreasing lower? prefix min remains lower
434    hi=np.maximum.accumulate(self.z.hi[::-1])[::-1]
435    i1=np.searchsorted(-low,-z.hi,side=’left’)
436    i2=np.searchsorted(-hi,-z.lo,side=’right’)-1
437    ok=(i1<len(kg.lo))&(i2>=0)
438    i1=np.clip(i1,0,len(low)-1);i2=np.clip(i2,0,len(low)-1)
439    klow=np.where(ok,kg.lo[i1],np.where(z.lo>0,0,kg.lo[-1]))
440    khigh=np.where(ok,kg.hi[i2],np.where(z.lo>0,kg.hi[0],1))
441    # even if ok at extreme include tail if i1==0 or i2==last
442    klow=np.where(i1==0,0,klow);khigh=np.where(i2==len(low)-1,1,khigh)
443    assert np.all(klow<=khigh)
444    k=I(klow,khigh)
445    l=1-k;l=I(np.maximum(0,l.lo),np.minimum(1,l.hi))
446    # lower V via 32
447    vl=rat(9,7)*k*l*l.sqrt()
448    return k,vl.lo
449   def checkH(self,da,db,delta):
450    sel=(da[’z’].lo<12)&(da[’z’].hi>-12)
451    z=da[’z’].sel(sel)
452    mi=I(da[’M’].lo,db[’M’].hi).sel(sel)
453    ki,vl=self.possiblek(z)
454    for d,g,upper in [(da,self.a,True),(db,self.b,False)]:
455     q,qp,k,l=[d[x].sel(sel) for x in [’Q’,’qp’,’k’,’l’]]
456     damp=.5-qp-g.a*k-(self.gap*ki if upper else 0)
457     br=mi*(qp-g.a*l)-ki*q+I(vl)
458     if upper:br-=g.a*ki*delta.sel(sel)
459     val=br+ERR*damp
460     assert np.min(damp.lo)>.06,np.min(damp.lo)
461     assert np.min(val.lo)>0,(g.A,np.min(val.lo),z.lo[np.argmin(val.lo)])
462    # tail q @ z <= -5.5 numerical
463    for d in [da,db]:
464     s=(d[’z’].lo<-5.5)
465     assert np.min(d[’Q’].lo[s])>-.28
466    assert self.z.hi[-1]<-5.5 and self.z.lo[0]>4
467    # delta tail <= X_b(-5.5) for negative z; numerical
468    s=db[’z’].lo<-5.5
469    assert np.max(db[’X’].hi[s])<.03
470    assert self.logp.hi[-1]<5
471    for g,arr in [(self.a,self.va0),(self.b,self.vb0)]:
472     assert -arr[0].hi[-1]*2*(.5-g.af)>2 and arr[0].lo[0]>6
473   def coarse(self,test,upper,detail=False):
474    test=iv(test)
475    slope=self.sl if upper else self.sh
476    p=iexp(anchored(slope))
477    v=self.vhi if upper else self.vlo


                                                      130
478   integrand=p*(v-2*test*kg*kg*lg/v)
479   # sign cumulative, allow tail error absolute 1e-7, only when away far endpoint
480   if upper:
481    # g negative near k=1 verify central tail say k>.95, analytical beyond cut
482    far=kg.lo>.95
483    if np.max(integrand.hi[far])>=0:return False
484    cum=partialsum(integrand,True)
485    val=np.max(cum.hi[~far])+1e-7
486    # positive tail k->0 total error
487    return val if detail else val<0
488   else:
489    far=kg.hi<.05
490    if np.min(integrand.lo[far])<=0:return False
491    cum=partialsum(integrand)
492    val=np.min(cum.lo[~far])-1e-7
493    return val if detail else val>0
494 @lru_cache(maxsize=3)
495 def refdata(g):
496  z,m,x,v=g.inverse(kg,lg)
497  q=Rmq(g,zcells(g)[’Q’]).query(z)
498  slope=-q/v; lp=anchored(slope); p=iexp(lp)
499  return z,m,x,v,q,slope,lp,p
500 def nonlineartest(env,test,upper,nt=8):
501  test=iv(test);c=rat(3,10)
502  gr=profiles(293650)
503  z,mr,xr,vr,q,sr,lpr,pr=refdata(gr)
504  tk=kg*lg
505  base=total(pr/vr*(vr.sq()+c*(mr*xr-tk)-2*test*kg*tk))
506  # actual slopes bounded, reference slope cell intervals
507  eps=ilog(I(env.vlo.lo,env.vhi.hi)/vr)
508  pis=env.slope-sr
509  pi=anchored(pis) # tighter than logp - lpr
510  out=base
511  for it in range(nt):
512   t=I((it)/nt,(it+1)/nt) # nt power2
513   vf=iexp(t*eps);pf=iexp(t*pi)
514   v=vr*vf;p=pr*pf
515   # cap v,p actual envelope geometric? both ref within => yes slope convex for interpolated log
516   v=v.cap(env.vlo.lo,env.vhi.hi)
517   pbox=iexp(env.logp);p=p.cap(pbox.lo,pbox.hi)
518   pv=p/v
519   inv=1/vf-1
520   def prim(ref,weight,rev):
521    # omitted tail |1/vfac-1|<=3 from heat ratio <4
522    tail=I(-3.01,3.01)*ref.sel(-1 if rev else 0)
523    y=ref+tail+partialsum(weight/vr*inv,rev)
524    static=(env.x if rev else env.m)
525    return y.cap(static.lo,static.hi)
526   m=prim(mr,kg,False);x=prim(xr,lg,True)
527   signed=c*(m*x-tk)-2*test*kg*tk
528   gp=pv*(v.sq()+signed)
529   gv=pv*(v.sq()-signed)-c*kg/v*partialsum(pv*x,True)-c*lg/v*partialsum(pv*m)
530   varv=total(gv*eps)
531   # omitted M epsilon derivative tails <=6 M_ref(cut), heat/log ratio
532   terror=6*(mr.hi[0]*total(c*pv*x).hi+xr.hi[-1]*total(c*pv*m).hi)
533   assert terror<3e-7,terror # may pv*m divergent near tail but finite cut logs polynomial!
534   coeff=choose(np.arange(len(width))<mid,-partialsum(gp),partialsum(gp,True))
535   varp=total(coeff*pis)
536   alt=total(gp*pi)
537   varp=varp.cap(alt.lo,alt.hi)
538   out+=(varv+varp)/nt
539  val=out.hi+1e-6 if upper else out.lo-1e-6
540  return val
541 def kquery(vals,i1,i2):
542  # range extrema sparse using generic
543  los=vals.lo;his=vals.hi
544  n=i2-i1+1
545  outlo=np.empty(len(n));outhi=np.empty(len(n))
546  step=1
547  while step<=max(n):
548   sel=(n>=step)&(n<2*step)
549   a=i1[sel];b=i2[sel]-step+1


                                                      131
550   outlo[sel]=np.minimum(los[a],los[b]);outhi[sel]=np.maximum(his[a],his[b])
551   los=np.minimum(los[:-step],los[step:]);his=np.maximum(his[:-step],his[step:])
552   step*=2
553  return I(outlo,outhi)
554 def localcheck():
555  E=Env(293650,293655)
556  a=I(E.a.a.lo,E.b.a.hi); d=rat(1,100)
557  k,l=kg,lg; z=E.z;m=E.m;x=E.x
558  assert -20<z.lo[-1] and z.hi[-1]<-10 and z.lo[0]>7 and z.hi[0]<20
559  W=I(E.vlo.lo,E.vhi.hi)
560  c0,c1,c2,c3=[rat(t,100) for t in [26,130,-186,325]]
561  L=c0+k*(c1+k*(c2+k*c3));Lp=c1+2*k*c2+3*k*k*c3;Lpp=2*c2+6*k*c3
562  assert np.max(L.hi)<3
563  t=k*l
564  T=2*(.5-a)*m*l/W-2*k+2*a*(t/W).sq()
565  B=.5*z+a*m
566  Wk=2*B-2*a*t/W
567  R=1+I(-1,1)*d*L
568  core=.5*W.sq()*Lpp+W*Wk*Lp
569  rhs=R.sq()*core-(a+I(-1,1)*d)*t*Lp+R*(-(R+1)*(.5+a*T)*L+I(0,T.maxabs())) # absolute adverse source
570  core0=I(-1,1)*d*core-(.5+a*T)*R
571  assert np.max(rhs.hi)<0,np.max(rhs.hi)
572  assert np.max(core0.hi)<0
573  epsmax=L/(1-d*L)
574  def lprim(weight,ref,rev):
575   val=I(0,3.11)*ref.sel(-1 if rev else 0)+partialsum(weight/W*epsmax,rev)
576   return I(0,np.minimum(val.hi,3.11*ref.hi))
577  lm=lprim(k,m,False);lx=lprim(l,x,True);lz=lm+lx
578  zi=z+I(-1,1)*d*lz
579  da=zcells(E.a);db=zcells(E.b)
580  delta=(db[’M’]-da[’M’]).cap(0,30)
581  Qh=da[’Q’]+E.a.a*delta+ERR*E.gap;Ql=db[’Q’]-E.b.a*delta-ERR*E.gap
582  Qsp=I(Ql.lo,np.minimum(0,Qh.hi))
583  rq=Rmq(E.a,Qsp)
584  Qstar=rq.query(z)
585  shifts=lz*I(rq.query(zi).maxabs()+2*.011) # pad
586  # H same z
587  sel=(da[’z’].lo<12)&(da[’z’].hi>-12); zz=da[’z’].sel(sel)
588  def poss(box,useW=False):
589   low=np.minimum.accumulate(box.lo);hi=np.maximum.accumulate(box.hi[::-1])[::-1]
590   i1=np.searchsorted(-low,-zz.hi,side=’left’);i2=np.searchsorted(-hi,-zz.lo,side=’right’)-1
591   i1=np.clip(i1,0,len(low)-1);i2=np.clip(i2,0,len(low)-1)
592   kk=I(np.where(i1==0,0,k.lo[i1]),np.where(i2==len(low)-1,1,k.hi[i2]))
593   assert np.all(i2>=i1)
594   ww=kquery(W,i1,i2) # tail max <= .0001 below from (32)
595   return kk,ww
596  ks,_=poss(z);ki,wi=poss(zi)
597  Ms=I(da[’M’].lo[sel],db[’M’].hi[sel]);ls=1-ks
598  assert np.max((m*l/(.5-(a+d)*l)).hi)<.65
599  dm=rat(65,100)
600  Mi=Ms+I(-1,1)*dm*d
601  qq=Qsp.sel(sel)
602  qp=2*(.5*zz+a*Ms)*qq-qq.sq()+2*(.5-a*ks-a)
603  damp=.5-qp-a*ks-d*ki
604  # wi max tails use endpoint or simple .001 extra
605  br=Mi*(qp-a*ls)-ki*qq+I(0,(1.031*wi.hi+.0001))+I(-1,1)*a*ki*dm*d
606  assert np.min(damp.lo)>.1
607  gain=a*dm+(a*(1-a)*dm+I(br.maxabs()))/damp
608  assert np.max(gain.hi)<2,np.max(gain.hi)
609  # functional
610  cadd=rat(3,10)
611  sr=-Qstar/W;lpr=anchored(sr);pr=iexp(lpr)
612  zc=z.sel(mid)
613  assert zc.maxabs()<2
614  pimax=2*I((z-zc).maxabs())+shifts
615  out=0
616  for it in range(4):
617   td=d*(it+1)/4
618   pvf=iexp(I(-1,1)*td*pimax)
619   vf=iexp(I(-1,1)*td*epsmax)
620   v=W*vf;p=pr*pvf;pv=p/v
621   mt=(m+I(-1,1)*td*lm).cap(.968*m.lo,1.033*m.hi)


                                                     132
622   xt=(x+I(-1,1)*td*lx).cap(.968*x.lo,1.033*x.hi)
623   signed=cadd*(mt*xt-t)-2*a*k*t
624   gp=pv*(v.sq()+signed)
625   gv=pv*(v.sq()-signed)-cadd*k/v*partialsum(pv*xt,True)-cadd*l/v*partialsum(pv*mt)
626   cv=total(I(gv.maxabs())*epsmax)
627   terror=4*(m.hi[0]*total(cadd*pv*xt).hi+x.hi[-1]*total(cadd*pv*mt).hi)
628   assert terror<3e-7
629   coeff=choose(np.arange(len(width))<mid,partialsum(gp),partialsum(gp,True))
630   cp=total(I(coeff.maxabs())*2/W)+total(I(gp.maxabs())*shifts)
631   out+=(cv+cp)/4
632  den=2*total(pv*k*t)
633  assert den.lo>0
634  return (out.hi+2e-6)/den.lo
635 def checkA():
636  n=4096
637  ind=np.arange(1,n-1)
638  k=I(ind/n,(ind+1)/n);l=1-k;t=k*l
639  low=rat(9,7)*k*l*l.sqrt(); high=rat(6,5)*(t*t.sqrt()).sqrt()
640  b=2*(rat(6,10)-k); B=-rat(4,10)*t
641  # max endpoint term convex when b>=0, decreasing if b<0
642  edge=(b*low.sq()+2*B*low).hi
643  edge=np.maximum(edge,(b*high.sq()+2*B*high).hi)
644  ws=[low+(high-low)*I(i/128,(i+1)/128) for i in range(128)]
645  mini=100.
646  for aa in range(419,1024): # covers [.41,1]
647   a=I(aa/1024,(aa+1)/1024)
648   K=12*a*k-5*a-1-b.sq()+rat(8,10)*(1-2*k)
649   pos=(6*a.sq()-rat(16,100))*t.sq()
650   mins=np.minimum.reduce([(2*w.sq()+K*w-2*b*B+pos/w).lo for w in ws])
651   # last cell crude integrand >=-9; first likewise and endpoint |...| upper<.001
652   assert np.max(abs(mins))<9
653   cs=np.cumsum(mins[::-1])[::-1]/n-2e-9
654   bound=cs-np.maximum(0,mins)/n-9/n-edge
655   val=min(np.min(bound[ind<=2560]),cs[0]-18/n-.001)
656   mini=min(mini,val)
657   assert val>0,(aa,val,cs[0])
658  return mini
659
660 def driver():
661  global AD
662  assert checkA()>0
663  A,B=(0, 410000)
664  for an,bn in [(167000, 410000), (183000, 410000), (185000, 400000), (193000, 397000), (197000, 390000)
       ,→ , (203000, 386000), (207000, 381000), (211000, 377000), (215000, 373000), (218000, 369000),
       ,→ (220000, 370000)]:
665   e=Env(A,B); print(A,B,flush=True)
666   if an>A: assert e.coarse(rat(an,AD),False)
667   if bn<B: assert e.coarse(rat(bn,AD),True)
668   A,B=an,bn
669  A,B=(220000, 370000)
670  for an,bn in [(228000, 370000), (231000, 368000), (234000, 363000), (239000, 358000), (244000, 350000)
       ,→ , (251000, 341000), (258000, 332000), (266000, 324000), (272000, 316000), (277000, 311000),
       ,→ (281000, 307000), (283000, 304000), (285000, 303000)]:
671   e=Env(A,B); print(A,B,flush=True)
672   if an>A: assert nonlineartest(e,rat(an,AD),False)>0
673   if bn<B: assert nonlineartest(e,rat(bn,AD),True)<0
674   A,B=an,bn
675
676  assert localcheck()<.95
677  AD=10**7
678  for A in [2936533,2936535]:
679   _,_,lam=certified(A)
680   assert (lam-10>A*1000 if A==2936533 else lam+10<A*1000)
